I went on dancing in my grotesque disguise, but not before I told him: “I am lonely and miserable but I am wearing my last skin. Since you are almost face to face with the Gods do not abandon me.” In human language, this is called love.


Leonora Carrington


Ambivalence about responsibility for our own freedom does not mean we are stupid, self-destructive, incapable, or desirous of harm. It means we are human. And part of being human is not always wanting every moment of our lives to be a step on a long march toward emancipation and enlightenment. It also means contending with desires to circle or enter dark rooms.


Maggie Nelson




OceanofPDF.com































OceanofPDF.com















Once upon a time, Sarah fell asleep in a castle and woke up in Sarah’s kitchen.


It was an age-old awakening.


This was where Sarah lived.


There was a man, Trevor, in their kitchen, talking.


In her hands were a wet rag and a plate.


In his hands were air and history.


In her hands were water and invisibility and time.


Sarah was a woman who lived in the kitchen and Trevor was a man who knew how to be in a kitchen talking to a woman.


He ate what she ate.


She ate what she made.


Once a week he cooked and she did the dishes afterward.


Most of the time she cooked and she did the dishes afterward.


They had two children, though once there were none.


Once it had been just the two of them and Trevor had liked that very much.


He had liked it when Sarah was his alone.


Their children would not eat beans, though the woman tried consistently, waiting for the moment this would change.


He would eat anything; sometimes it made him sick, sometimes he appreciated it.


He told her she was very good at holding a wet rag; he admired how efficient she was with it.


It was so much faster for her to do the dishes.




———


A scene played out with slight variations time and time again.


The dance of domestic estrangement repeated in the same disappointing ways.


General, and so particular.




———


Sarah’s hands were soppy. She was holding a wet rag. Pursing her lips and scowling because it smelled goddamn awful.


She had been trying to concentrate on the warm water, the patterns on the plates, the rhythmic motions, humming Bruce Springsteen’s “Dancing in the Dark” as a way to be present in her liveliness, her duty, and the joy that might be taken in these things despite the tiredness. She had intentionally manifested this—Sarah’s private dance party—happily inhabiting Sarah in the kitchen.


Someone had posted it on her feed. A quote from a renowned Buddhist teacher about all work being sacred duty, dependent on one’s quality of mind.


Sarah wanted a high-quality mind.


She was working on feeling joy for what she was doing instead of resenting that she was always the one doing it, swaying her body to the inner song, tap, tap, tapping her feet and popping up her shoulders like Bruce—a little smile playing at the corners of her lips. She would write about this after, headline it 
“Dish Dancing!”
 Complete with a playlist curating a perfect dish dancing soundtrack. Should 
she also choreograph a dance routine and make a video of that? No, too much. She didn’t have time to choreograph a dance routine tonight! Moms didn’t have time to learn a choreographed dance routine for doing dishes, it would just be more work!


It had to be spontaneous. She mentally updated her headline: 
“Dish Dancing on the Spot!”
 It was perfect! She could even include a small quip about her favourite scrubby with tips on how to spot-scrub baked-on grease while dancing! Get commission with an affiliate link, some free samples for herself. God, she loved her job!


Then Trevor had walked into the room to talk about a historical shift in consciousness. Which he talked about often. It was a legitimate fear. But not anything she could do anything about 
atm
. She had her own thought stream going, and she wanted to dance!


He had interrupted her dance to talk about how hard it was all becoming, how uncertain he was about his place in the world, whether any of it had any meaning. How distant his students seemed now, how unresponsive, whereas before he had felt so close to them.


He was trying to connect, or rather looking for someone—anyone—to listen to what he had to say. Sarah knew it had nothing to do with her, specifically. He just needed an audience.


She didn’t want to give her space up, yet again, to his ever-enduring existential misery.


She’d been dancing!


For a split second, had thought to share her joy with him—on the verge of inviting him to dance with her, until she saw the look on his face.


Shoulder-popping smile swiftly transformed to jaw clamp, shutting down her system so she would not be drawn into the anxiety-with-no-reprieve dance of Trevor’s. The only dance he seemed to know the moves to anymore in the home they shared—but open the door to the outside world and the man was all amiable smiles.


All her energy drained into the greasy water, balled in the fists of her soppy hands. He broke in before she had a chance to extend the invitation. “It’s the encroachment of death, Sarah. I feel it coating my throat, like a sickness that never ends. I think I might be coming down with the girls’ cold—another time-fuck, more days of work I can’t afford to miss. Feels so pointless sometimes.”


Compassion, Sarah, compassion
, suggested the mindful voice in her head. She sighed, releasing her fists. “It’s hard, Trev, I know. Feels like a lot. I’ve just spent an hour putting the girls to bed and I’d like to finish these dishes so I can get my post up before I collapse. I just got this great idea while I was doing the dishes that I know will really resonate.”


“I feel like I’m on the edge here, Sarah! And you want to talk about your blog? I felt it in my heart just now—maybe something serious? Down my throat straight into my heart. Can you touch? Do you feel palpitations? Should 
we call the nurse’s line?” He was getting frantic. She needed to calm the frantic before he really built it up.


“Trev, come on, it’s too much. It’s the kids’ cold. I had it a few days ago, and now you’re getting it. Give it a few days to run its course. Your heart is fine, maybe you have a little fever? Stick the thermometer in your mouth and check, if you’re worried. Besides, life’s not going to fall apart if you rest for a couple of days. It would probably do you good. Maybe give you the headspace to prioritize so it doesn’t feel so overwhelming.”


“You’re not taking me seriously, Sarah! My life is going to shit and all you’re worried about is the house, the kids, or your trivial posts about doing the dishes, as if these are important things in the world when there are wildfires threatening to engulf us all, wars and famines, and my heart is about to give out,” he retorted, then huffed to the couch, collapsing with the greatest fanfare, tossing his arm over his eyes, letting out small communicative moans that Sarah used her well-honed jaw-clamping technique to block out while she finished the dishes.


Trivial? Compassion! Trivial? Did he help me out when I was sick with the cold?
 “He doesn’t mean it!” she thought. “He never means it. He’s just upset right now. Compassion! He just wants attention. You need to give him more attention.” 
Maybe if he did the dishes sometimes, I wouldn’t have heart palpitations every time he asked for my attention!




———


Getting nothing from Sarah, Trevor took himself to the bedroom.


Sarah touched her hands to her stomach and breathed big deep belly breaths to calm herself. She would not let herself be drawn into it tonight.


When she opened her eyes, something inside offered up a few lines of classic Beyoncé about love getting her looking crazy.


She knew Beyoncé was singing about a different kind of crazy, not the hair-pulling, sopping stinking rag, leaden feet, resentful, tired kind of crazy flooding in, wrenching her out of her flow.


She forced a smile. 
Keep at it, Sarah.
 Something to be grateful for: the playlist was bubbling up out of the dishwater—Bruce and Beyoncé’s dance moves intermingling in a musical daydream. And then she pictured it: her wet rag like the exploding fire hydrant Beyoncé kicks into a shower in response to the flames of Jay-Z’s burning car—an unhappiness intervention to get her groove back while Trevor’s dejection dragged at her feet, wanting her to respond to his fears with appropriate care, tend to his anxieties, just cheer him up a little. Redirect her flow to bring him out of his constantly churning unhappy mind whenever he needed a pickup so that he wouldn’t be left out in the cold. Knowing that Sarah would always be there with her eternal wellspring, taking care of him, like she’d always done.


It was the least she could do, being a woman in a kitchen.




———


What was wrong with her? Why was she being so godawful to him? She must be PMSing. Maybe needed to get to more yoga, up her meditation to fifteen minutes? She needed to nip this irritability in the bud so it didn’t get the better of her.


She went into the bedroom and offered Trevor a glass of ice water. Apologized for not listening when he needed her to, teasing her hands into his hair, soothing him with a gentle head massage, leaving the blog post unwritten.

Trevor went to sleep in his empty marriage bed after masturbating to his favourite porn video and woke up in his university bed holding a worn paperback of Bukowski’s 
Play the Piano Drunk Like a Percussion Instrument
 in his right hand, stiff cock in his left, stroking, imagining it was Ariadne’s hand.


Then he felt it—her hand replacing his, her lips, whispering in his ear, her body there, beside him, still half dreaming! “Ariadne,” he moaned, and the hand slipped away, the warm breath leaving a spit of cold air on his earlobe.


He opened his eyes—Sarah.


“In your dreams,” Sarah said. “Just me—one of these days you’ll stop hoping.”


“Fuck, Sarah, I’m sorry. I didn’t mean…”


“You did, but it’s okay. It’s a process. I think about her all the time too. Maybe it’s just easier for me because I could never mistake you for her, so I’ll take it as a compliment.”


“Keep going?” he asked.


“Say something nice to me first—something about me.”


Trevor released the book, brought his hand to Sarah’s head and gripped her thick curly hair. She loved it when he did that. She let out a sigh. He kissed her forehead, moved his lips to her ear, breathed her name there, “Sarah.”


And then again, “Sarah.”


“You, Sarah, you. You are mine. My Sarah. My moon-eyed girl, my beautiful sweetheart. I love hearing your lips say my name. I want to wake up and feel your body next to 
mine every morning for the rest of my life. It’s you. You, you, you, you, you.”


Which did the trick.


Sarah slid her hand back to its proper place and wrapped her legs around him, her wetness hot against his thigh.


“Trevor! Trevor! Trehhh-vooohrh! Can you get out here and give me a hand please?”


Sarah, frantic.


“Now!


“I’ve got the baby on the breast and Amelie’s just taken a dump in the potty and is running around with half of it still all over her cheeks!


“Now!


“Before it gets. All. Over. The. House!”


Trevor sighed. “Coming!” Though he hadn’t, quite yet. Back in his marriage bed, after all.




OceanofPDF.com















Masha went to sleep in her mother’s arms and woke up alone in a hospital incubator, wailing for a breast she’d never receive.


She hated this dream. She wanted another, so she woke herself up.


Found herself in her room in Prague, remembering the shoot was today. She felt giddy. No going back to sleep now. She got up and did some stretches, flushed with a desire to go up on pointe.


Today was the day. Was she ready? It was going to be intense. Gorgeous, intense. She was prepared, but there was so much feeling one couldn’t predict, and the unanticipated gestures that might arise from that. She had gone through the choreography so many times, had the piece memorized. Had done the work needed to prepare with the other performers. They, too, knew their roles. She brought her hands together, as if holding a large ball of thread with several loose ends, which she pretended to cast in all directions. Flopped back down on the bed and put her headphones on, curled up with her pillow and closed her eyes, drifting into character with the first few notes of Jessye Norman singing “Es gibt ein Reich.”




OceanofPDF.com















Yuna awoke in the darkness of pre-dawn, blue just beginning. A body slipping through darkness, into an imaginal world. They felt their heart thump with the blood of all thumping hearts. Time a continuum of rushing water, singing. They would tell the one they loved of this beauty. With their words, made from the blood of time. Though how much truer to tell wordless, heart to thumping heart.




OceanofPDF.com















Ariadne Awoke


by Sarah Perkins


Once upon a time Ariadne went to sleep on an island, wrapped in the threads of a myth.


As though for thousands of years she’d been spinning in a captivating story.


Her destiny hinged on falling in love with a hero and offering him a clew to win his devotion—a ball of red thread to lead him out of the labyrinth after his deed was done, from darkness back to light.


His task was to kill the monster within. An aberration made manifest by genius costuming—Ariadne’s mother having cosplayed a heifer to fulfill her god-induced desire—thereafter birthing a half-bull son.


In gifting Theseus an escape, Ariadne had betrayed her family, rendering herself homeless. Her future now in Theseus’s hands.


A gift of string. A string of words.


A hero’s pledge.


A woman’s destiny.


Ariadne, believing in love and the future offered in return—a beloved’s words, akin to the quickening within.




———


Telling between word and deed is tricky. Myths diverge, though most agree that Theseus emerged from the labyrinth victorious after killing the Minotaur, sailing off—as promised—with Ariadne. Stopping off at some point on the island of Naxos.


What happened between getting on the ship and stopping 
off on Naxos is anybody’s guess, but given a beautiful lovestruck girl away from home for the first time in the company of a hero with convincing words, probably a lot of sex.


They disembark, and Ariadne falls asleep sweetly in the arms of her lover. When she awakes, he is gone. Along with his ship.


And here is where the variations begin:




———


Ariadne, after discovering she’s been abandoned by her one true love, hangs herself.




———


Ariadne, after discovering she’s been abandoned by her one true love, lives through nine months of devastation, then dies birthing Theseus’s twins.




———


Ariadne is abandoned by Theseus, not through his own will, but through the force of Dionysus, deity of pleasure and loosened inhibitions, who wants Ariadne for himself.




———


Ariadne, devastated after she discovers she’s been abandoned, contemplates killing herself, but is rescued by Dionysus, who consoles her with his godly love.




———


Ariadne is killed by the goddess Artemis, rescued from the land of the dead by Dionysus, and made immortal, birthing Dionysus’s immortal sons.




———


In a world of mortals, gods, and myth, Ariadne had a part to play.


But what did Ariadne desire? And what power did she have to make what she desired real?




———


Ariadne felt lost. She was sharing a dorm room with a stranger who whispered in the night, in a city in which she knew no one, and her hair was a mess because her roommate had claimed her hairbrush. Not to mention that she hadn’t done laundry in two weeks because she had not attended the orientation on laundry and didn’t have the energy to try to find the laundry room, let alone come up with the quarters she needed to run the machines.


Ariadne’s mother had been dead four years, but Ariadne felt her presence, her thoughts sometimes filled with her mother’s voice saying things like: “Go ask Housing Services for information about laundry, Ariadne, you are running out of underwear. It’s up to you to write your own destiny. You can begin by cashing that ten-dollar bill in for a roll of quarters.”


Sometimes Ariadne felt trapped inside her own mind—a maze of thoughts, sensations, feelings, and only her uncertain inclinations or a ghost’s to guide her; her father too lost in his own grief to offer anything that made sense. “Tomorrow, Mom, tomorrow. I promise. I don’t have the energy today.” And then “You will be alone forever, forever, forever. She is gone, gone, gone.”


Ariadne did not want her mother to be dead or to be alone in the world. Ariadne did not want to write her own destiny. Sometimes she wanted someone else to do things for her, so she didn’t always have to do them herself. She didn’t want a destiny. She wanted her mother back.


But in this life, Ariadne had no divine husband who would go to the land of the dead, find her mother, and bring her back to the land of the living to pull a roll of quarters from her purse.


She had no choice, really, but to write her own story, because if she didn’t, she’d get kicked out of university and have to go back home to live with her grieving father and have to do his laundry too. Or end up on the street, and then she really didn’t know what she’d do for clean clothes.


So where should she begin? Should she eat shellfish for breakfast? Should she go for a run? What should she wear today? Anything! Anything she wanted! Though choices were getting desperately low. “The world is your oyster,” the ghost in her mind told her. Also, it told her, “Get out there, girl! Go live ‘your one precious wild life.’ ”


Ariadne hated it when the ghost misquoted Mary Oliver, though she’d loved it when her mom had read Oliver to her.


What Ariadne really had to do was get her ass out of bed and get to her Intro to Anthropology class, which started in thirty minutes. Fine. A good way to start a destiny. A real alternative that didn’t hinge on the moment she would finally fall in love with a heroic Athenian youth who had a destiny greater than hers, but who needed her love to fulfill it, after which she was negotiable; her destiny decided by everyone but herself.


Good thing she was in Victoria, fat chance of any heroic Athenian youths here.


She had a greater destiny at hand.




———


Sarah couldn’t believe she still had it! The story she had written in university almost twenty years ago! A moment in time she had imagined for Ariadne, her new best friend: getting out of bed, making her way to the class where, instead of meeting a hero, she would finally meet Sarah! A new beginning that would save them both from being lost in the obscure destinies grief had cast upon them—reawakening Ariadne to life from the loss of her mother, and Sarah to playful imagination in the wake of her father’s dark death.


She wondered if Ariadne remembered this story. Ariadne would not be surprised that Sarah still had it because Sarah still had everything. Her archives were immaculate—her notebooks meticulously organized in chronologically ordered, methodically stacked plastic totes, labelled with the cool vintage label maker she and Ariadne had picked up on one of their thrifting expeditions, tastefully arranged on industrial-grade steel shelving in Sarah and Trevor’s garage.


How time flew!


Sarah, with the records to show for it.


Here she was, looking up ancient files, researching a blog post on best practices for archiving the family—the label maker intervening, drawing Sarah back into the real world, reminding Sarah that she was almost out of the gold sticker tape she needed, reserved exclusively now for labelling her notebook totes. What would she do when it ran out? She made a mental note to start looking on eBay for 
vintage sellers who might have stock before she found herself in a labelling emergency!


How many times had she had this same thought and not started looking? Sarah was slipping. Really slipping. She should be on top of these things! Maybe she needed to revamp her to-do list approach? She’d see if any of her blogging moms had new content for prioritizing to-do lists that fell into different life categories and how she might properly categorize this one so it didn’t always fall off the list. These were her lifelong notebooks! What would happen if she had to switch to another labeller? Would it knock her completely off-brand? And then who would she even be, Sarah wondered, interrupted by “Mommy! Mooooomy!!!”—Arlynn waking up from her nap upstairs in the bedroom.




OceanofPDF.com































OceanofPDF.com

















Chapter One


Childhood Snapshots




The Chick


“Papa! Papa, look!” Sarah ran, cupping the newly hatched chick in her hands. Holding its preciousness ever gentle, up against her heart. Looking down at the chick instead of closely at Anthony as she ran across the yard toward him.


Anthony was down at the truck, tinkering.


She should have known better.


If she’d looked. If she’d listened first. If she’d not been in such a rush.


“The goddamn fucking piece of shit truck, better to light the fucking thing on fire,” Anthony was muttering to himself as he tried to pry loose a seized bolt, the fingers on his left hand greasy and slipping as he started to slam the 
wrench in his right hand harder and harder, stripping the bolt, ripping up his knuckles.


“Câlisse de merde! Éstis de câlisse de crisse de tabarnak! Maudit de junk! Piece of fucking shit!” he shouted, jerking upright, flinging the wrench as hard as he could, bringing his knuckles to his mouth.


If she hadn’t been so excited about the chick.


If she hadn’t been anticipating it—the egg she’d incubated with a lightbulb near the fireplace, turning it three times a day for three weeks. Except the last four days when it had to be left still. Three whole weeks! Papa helping her set up the box. Lending her his shop lamp, putting up a crossbar with little hooks so she could move the lamp around and adjust the heat. Going around to the neighbour’s to get a hold of a thermometer with a humidity gauge, looking in on it every evening before bed to make sure Sarah hadn’t forgotten anything.


If she’d had her head up instead of down, she would have seen the wrench, maybe dodged it.


He hadn’t heard her over his mutters. Hadn’t looked before flinging the wrench. It had been an accident.


The wrench hit her hands, breaking three of her fingers, squishing them in on the chick. She stumbled, trying to keep the chick safe, to not fall on top, not yet realizing it was already dead. So she fell headfirst, cheek turned sideways, smashed her left temple on the corner of Anthony’s metal toolbox.


Bloody knuckles in his mouth, running over to where she’d just fallen, not realizing the wrench had hit, Anthony cursing, “You crazy, sauvage girls! Just like your câlisse de mère! Be careful! Never give me one goddamn break for one goddamn minute. Fucking sows can’t fucking think for one second. Can’t you see? Oh fuck. Oh shit”—seeing the wrench he’d just thrown next to his daughter’s crumpled body—“what have I done? Baby? Baby? Marbles


As a boy, Trevor collected marbles. He would sit by the window, holding his favourite ones up to the light, turning them between his fingers, rolling them in his palm. Cat’s eyes in orange, blue, red, and mixed green and yellow, making him feel like he had been pounced on by some creature outside the bonds of family and all the incessant rules he was always butting heads with at the marble ring with the boys at school. How badly he wanted their prized marbles, how furious he became when they won his—an anger so deep he was banned from playing.


The school called home asking that Trevor no longer bring his marbles, since the other kids had accused him of stealing. He wasn’t stealing, he was just taking his marbles back, he told his mother. She tried to explain that this was not how the game worked. But Trevor didn’t care about the rules of the game, he just wanted the marbles—his and the other kids’, because the other kids could not feel the light inside the way Trevor did.


The two he was most transfixed by were his aquamarine and blue taws. They were the most beautiful—the one, and then the other—his devotion pulled back and forth between 
them. His sister Mimi had given him the aquamarine, which she’d told him was a little water planet. He moved beyond form inside this marble, dissolving into a pre-human amalgam. Later, watching 
Deep Space Nine
, he would witness Odo joining himself to the mercurial lake of his species’ unseparated wholeness, and feel that same jolt of longing he had felt turning the aquamarine taw in the light.


The deep-blue taw was something different. He’d taken it from Mimi’s room without asking and pretended he didn’t know where it was when she accused him of stealing it. Like space, like beyond, like dreams that could never turn to nightmare. He could not quite see into its centre—an always-hidden place. A place he was drawn toward and terrified by. He never looked into the blue for too long because it disoriented him. Made him fear he would get stuck and never be able to get out. His as long as he could manage the thrill of keeping it hidden away when it rightfully belonged to another.


His steelies he loved to tap on the window. Turn and tap. Tap, tap, tap, the tiniest sound. Not loud enough that his father could hear and yell from downstairs to stop the incessant noise. He liked to feel the weight of them in his palm, a coldness different from the glass cold of his other marbles. A different kind of power—the steelies could break a window if thrown, he learned in the schoolyard, after which his father took the steelies away. Threatening to take all of his marbles if there were any more problems.




———


Trevor awoke the next day and took a hammer to them all. Smashing them to pieces, sweeping up the shards and dumping them in the trash before his father could find the wreckage. All but the deep-blue taw, which he hid away in the recesses of his closet, vowing he would never look at it again. Bar it from his mind so his father would not suspect he had anything precious at all.


What use were precious things if there were other, bigger, stronger men to take the things you loved away? Who would inevitably come and claim them as their own, or deny you access, letting you know you did not deserve them.


By wrecking them, he made sure no one could take them away. The secret of the beauty they had once announced, his alone to keep. To marvel at, to covet, to lock away. To annihilate if he so desired.


Sublunar


Sarah had once believed the moon existed as a secret only for her. It was not that other people couldn’t see the moon, or feel its pull, or be dazzled beyond reason by its power. Rather, it was that she understood the moon had a secret language, and was able to converse openly with it as though the moon were her BFF orbiting the earth.


Sarah felt that the moon often felt misunderstood, and that she, Sarah, offered the moon a kind of attention that the moon secretly longed for. Finding it only in the rarest of humans who were gifted with sublunar insight into the realms of the moon’s orbit and influence.


Once, Sarah’s father had told her, most people had been this way—when they had lived so much more of their lives under open skies. But now, because of light pollution and rooftops, and all the trappings that defined human lives, the moon had lost much of its influence, becoming, for most, simply symbolic. A circle on wall calendars that changed shape throughout the month, though the form in the sky the shapes represented was rarely, if ever, seen. Let alone sensed and understood through all of the body’s knowing.


For much of Sarah’s pre-adult life, the moon’s influence made the preoccupations of other humans mostly unimportant to her. To Sarah, it seemed as though most people were only concerned with the surfaces of their lives, identifying themselves by what they gathered—unaware of their phasing in the variegations of light cast by the moon upon their earthly skin. Sarah, meanwhile, could see the energy fields arising in response to the moon’s entreaties, rippling just under the skin of other humans, and beasts, and plant matter—enlivened by waves borrowed from the sun, and from other, more ancient stars beyond.


It was all very gorgeous. But it made friendships with other children difficult.


Until Sarah went to school and learned to discipline her thinking. Learned, in fact, that the cosmic languages she was listening to were fabrications of an overactive imagination. That none of it was logically real.


Sarah had a good long while, however, with her fantastic ability to hear what the moon was whispering, because 
she was homeschooled. Her mother encouraged devotion to the stars, and the moon, and moon snails, and the prickly skin of wild hazelnuts as authoritative sources of knowledge. Sarah did not go to school until she was fifteen, begging her parents to allow her to attend the high school in the nearest town to the homestead she was raised on.


The sudden plunge into sociality proved both awkward and revelatory to Sarah, who was busy looking for every other student’s light, while they were all mostly busy trying to corral that light into the most conventional forms possible so as not to shine out of the bounds of their expected containers. They were meant to illuminate certain geometrical forms, Sarah learned—forms which conformed to the particular shapes and hues of the bodies they had been born into, replicas of the normative ideals epitomized in the short-lived power coupling of Brad Pitt and Gwyneth Paltrow, an idealized matching so potent that the couple still hovered years later in Sarah’s psyche as a Fate that should have been, but wasn’t, and only if it had, if that one couple had stuck, then maybe the world would have turned out differently than it did—shattered into so many terrifying news clips. She had learned about them at school from the other girls and had reasoned that the only way she would ever find belonging was to find out everything about them that she possibly could, so the other girls would think she was cool and the boys would see her as worth paying attention to.


She had used her money from selling surplus eggs to 
buy a poster of them, which, much to her mother’s dismay, Sarah kept on the wall above her bed, just at the spot above her sleeping head where the moon’s light would shine in her window on clear nights of fullness, conversing with both Sarah and her idols at the same time.


Sarah feared, when she learned of the couple’s breakup, that she had somehow been involved. That she had failed to do something to properly idolize them, thereby causing a cosmic split in the sublunar realm that made their differences irreconcilable. A thing for which she could never forgive herself. Probably it wasn’t her fault. Did she really have that kind of influence? Rational thought told her she was just one girl among potentially millions who idolized the couple, but those other girls did not have the moon’s power behind them like Sarah, and probably knew how to idolize in the right way, whereas she, Sarah, did not. She had loved them too much, or wrongly, and somehow the moon had felt their coupledom as too strong a cosmic force, unbearable, and so had teased its way through Sarah into the poster, somehow severing their bond.


Hex Key


Until he turned twelve, Trevor was fairly certain his father’s vocabulary consisted almost entirely of words in two general categories: things he approved of and things he disapproved of. Things he approved of were universally true. Things he disapproved of were errors and false worldviews held by people who were different from himself and therefore wrong.


Some who were wrong knew better and some didn’t—neither, in Trevor’s father’s opinion, deserved much from him other than complete disregard, or, given the chance to set them straight, correctives to set them back on course. Those who knew better would do what they could to correct whatever misfortune had prevented them from fitting into his worldview; those who did not would continue on living their corrupt lives.


As far as Trevor could tell, Walt’s favourite words were “redundancy,” “complacency,” “inefficiency,” “government changeover,” and “moral corruption.”


Most of Walt’s favourite words had to do with political and economic order. But “moral corruption,” Trevor understood, meant anything vaguely hinting at improper intimate relations between people. The only proper intimate relations being those that took place between husbands and wives behind closed doors, and mothers and children, within reason, during the given time mothers needed to be in close proximity to their children’s bodies in order to care for them.

Mastercraft
, hanging off a small hook under his work table.

Chapter Two


Under the Influence




Bukowski


His first week of university Trevor was partnered with Sean in mandatory Survey of English Literature—just his luck to be paired with the behemoth who dwarfed everyone in the room and likely could not speak more than a five-word sentence. A man who had likely done time and was now going back to school to reintegrate. Or maybe he’d recently been discharged from the army?


But no, the bruiser was only nineteen, just like Trevor. Though he wasn’t fresh from under his mother’s apron, calling home every day, like Trevor—sick in his stomach, berating himself until he fell into fitful sleep every night for following through on the insanity of attending university on the other side of the country, wondering how long 
he’d last with the cafeteria food that gave him unending diarrhea that would surely kill him before Thanksgiving, at which point his parents would have to take him back.


At the end of the second week, Sean invited Trevor to drink beer with him at the beach after class.


“Brewskies on the beach? I’ve got a six-pack in my bag.”


Trevor, on his way to chemistry, almost said no. The beers would be warm, wouldn’t they? It was only noon! Trevor had to get to the cafeteria and find something that wouldn’t make him shit before class started and have to get up three times to shit during class, and would he have to shit on the beach while they were drinking beers down there?


But then something slipped in between his brain and his mouth and different words came out.


“Beers, um?” he asked, lifting his wrist to look at his watch.


“Yeah, man, by the time we get there it’ll be after lunch. I’ve got a quinoa salad in my bag, too. Plenty for both of us. Even brought two forks.”


“What the fuck is quinoa?” Trevor thought to himself. And, “This guy eats salad for lunch?” Saying, “Yeah, fuck, why not, eh? Really. Why the fuck not?” Why the hell this guy would want to hang out with him made Trevor wonder if there was something wrong with Sean, given what a wreck Trevor was, but maybe he was just a friendly guy who’d hang out with anyone he met in class?

quickly did the generous thing that very tall men’s brains must do in the company of shorter men—barely a pause, like he was still on the upswing, swishing for a moment before lowering ever so slightly—not so low that it would be considered a low-five for him, which would carry with it a tinge of humiliation for the man who had to reach—and not so high that Trevor would have to jump—but just to that ideal height that would spell a perfect high-five for Trevor, where he could slap just as hard as he liked, sealing their camaraderie in the resonant thwack that echoed to all those within hearing range, an aural-tactile confirmation of collegial white male friendship solidified.

pretty sailboats anchor on bay,


big houses protrude like shafts


of concrete


from lush forest.
”




Then he chuckled, shot Trevor a half smile using only the left side of his mouth, and, as though all part of the same expression, took a swig, slipped off his steel-toed leather boots using only his heels, peeled off his socks using his toes, and dug his naked feet into the sand.

Almost engulfing it—bringing vital poetry into Trevor’s life where only a vague sense of numbness had been for so long Trevor could barely remember otherwise.

His mother understood when he told her, “Mom, I like having poets and writers in my head instead of just myself, it makes me feel less lonely.” She found a way to make his dad begrudgingly accept.


Almost a decade down the road, not yet envisioned as the course life would take from that first worn paperback, Trevor’s Ph.D. dissertation on Bukowski would be an embarrassment to Walter and Eileen Hart.


He would try to tell them not to bother reading it, that it wouldn’t be their thing, but they would insist, and he’d feel he owed them, seeing as their money had paid for most of his education—a rare English lit Ph.D. coming out the other end debt-free.


“I don’t read poetry, son,” was what Walt would say. “But you’re a smart kid, and I can tell you know your stuff. That man though, he makes no sense to me. He’s got no morals other than a commitment to himself as far as I can tell.”


“Well, he’s a dirty man, and a misogynist,” Eileen would offer. “It’s hard for me to like anything he has to say. Me and your father, we don’t live our lives that way. But I can see that he speaks to you, that there’s a rawness to him that could be appealing to young men. I just wish it seemed like he cared about something. He seems like he just wants to eat everything up forever and not clean up after himself. Like he’s not even aware of everyone else who will have to.”


Trevor would be surprised at his mother’s statements about Bukowski, because she 
did
 clean up after his father all the time, and had cleaned up after Trevor when he’d lived there, and she never outwardly complained about it. At least not to him—her life obscured for him by her ever-present role, blurred into that one impossible symbol, the eternal caregiver.


Despite pretending otherwise, Trevor had harboured a small delusional hope that his parents would be moved by his work. Imagining for a moment that they might truly see him, allowing themselves to glimpse Bukowski’s spirit as something alive in themselves, perhaps even gaining some small insight into how the whole façade of 
their
 lives was what the man was tearing apart by virtue of the way he lived his—made real by his raw gift of language. How grotesque it all was. How their bodies were there, and animal underneath. All the raging desires they had to suppress to be as they were.


His hope would be crushed, as it had been so many times, by the easiness of his father’s disapproval, and his mother’s unwillingness to see beyond the walls of her immaculate world.




———



				Though Trevor’s heartfelt desire was to shine in the world unequivocally as Bukowski’s literary offspring, he was, he would come to accept over the course of his poetic education, more his parents’ child than Bukowski’s literary progeny. Trevor was simply unsuited to living raw life with 
abandon. Yet he could find consolation in an intellectual superiority that made him able to see deeply into the game at play.


A game most people were stuck in without even realizing it! Which some won out of luck or circumstance, while others lost due to misfortune or odds tipped against them. And while Trevor lacked certain things, he had the fortitude, cleverness, and words to pull strings to turn the game to his favour.


Trevor had learned much from Bukowski, but he would find his own kind of freedom. He could not be Bukowski, nor could he be Sean. He had to make of Trevor what he could. His words, one day, to be carried in worn paperbacks in the pockets of other men.




———



				Though Bukowski had been the first and most formative, there were others Trevor treasured, memorizing lines as his own voice took form. Gems he discovered in the most unlikely places, alert to their otherwise hidden potencies, unearthed by his obsession for scouring the world for obscure lines. Left in his path, he knew, for him to transform to greatness. Books by unimportant, long-dead poets in second-hand shops; the foreign-language stacks in the university library where he pulled poetry books off shelves in random order to find English translations that would never have revealed themselves in the digital catalogue. A basement dive in a colonial neighbourhood frequented only by fledgling poets and elderly patrons where, one evening, 
as Trevor was deeply considering his virgin screwdriver, crunching an ice cube to shards between his teeth, twirling his tongue among the fragments, he heard a young woman’s voice from the mic.


Unusual. The slight hesitation in her voice gave Trevor pause. A brief show of nerves before something else took over. It was rare in this joint for a woman so young, likely a lowly undergrad like Trevor and Sean, to expose herself to the scrutiny of grad students and more established poets.


He looked up and was immediately enraptured in the shock of her cheekbones, the curvature of her lips as they parted, releasing an echo that subsumed the murmurs in the room in its otherworldly halo.


Ice exploded luminous on Trevor’s tongue as though birthing a cosmos into being—his body burst open into new realms of sensing as he fell into synesthesia—rendering him almost completely incapable of normal function.


He did not hear her speak her name.


All her words were light within him.


Possessing him entirely.


She held all meaning and made none.




———


This was not the drive he felt with most girls to peel their clothes off, find the secret place they kept, and fuck them there, if they let him.


He’d had some successes. The drink had helped with that. Now that he was sober, it was harder to slip off the inhibitions. But maybe this too was a sign? That his 
attention was not meant to be directed wholly at getting pretty girls naked, but for purer means.


Grace. Revelation. Undoing.


He was meant to get close to this one.


This singular one who was not a girl at all, but some kind of goddess descended from heaven.


Love Under the Influence


Sarah met Ariadne. And then she met Trevor.


Ariadne was the first person whose mind made visible to Sarah the beat of her own heart—she could almost see it thumping outside herself in resonant beats of silver, like moon glinting off snowflakes set in rhythm with Ariadne’s beating heart.


With Trevor, when the spark came, it burned, flaring blue within an orange halo, pulling her to its obscure core.


Sarah thrilled at this new allure in proximity to brilliant minds—her body and mind alight in response. Awake again to dreaming across realms into others’ dreams.


A feeling she had felt often enough from books, or from the forest and river in deep conversation. From the moon’s pull. Each sparking within her its own unique spell.




———



				Ariadne was a profusion of passions and curiosities that inspired Sarah’s imagination. A pull both inward and outward that Sarah had not felt since she’d begun busying herself trying to belong in a social world her father had 
done his best to keep her out of. Whatever tentative connections she had made in that world severed almost completely when he died the summer before her final year of high school, and Sarah dropped out to stay at home with her mother.


A year passed in the world, or one long day become night become day.


It was hard to tell time severed from humanity.


Sarah managed to complete her high school courses by correspondence and fill out university applications because her mother threatened to sell the homestead and move them both to the city if Sarah did not. Sarah had refused, but Grace insisted that if Sarah did not go to university on her own, Grace would apply for her, then rent them a dismal one-bedroom basement apartment where she would sit at the window sipping chamomile tea, grief-stricken and miserable, dragging Sarah to classes, where she would sit behind her and murmur to herself the whole time, afterward dragging her home for late nights listening to Joni Mitchell’s 
Blue
 album on repeat, forcing Sarah to bear not only her own sorrow, but also Grace’s lifelong harassment. Grace, not one to make idle threats, had been convincing enough that Sarah had carried through on her demands.


Now here Sarah was on an island in the Salish Sea, attending university. Without her mother.


She felt here, but not here. Grace could force her out, but she could not force her to presence.


Sarah wanted to try, but she felt stuck, like her father had taken her future with him to the grave. As though some part of her were rotting under the earth while the unfeeling parts of her went on enacting the life she was supposed to be living.


Over the phone, Grace assured her that even though Sarah could not feel it now, there was still hope alive in her somewhere that would one day make itself known once the part under the earth decomposed, pushed to the surface in worm casings, absorbed into new life cycles of sunflowers or dried to dust in the summer sun, blown by an insistent wind into airborne debris, drifting up to the reaches of the atmosphere before finally returning to the stars.




———


Ariadne reawakened Sarah’s aliveness. Fate peeling itself open with the perfect opening line.




———


Sarah had arrived at her first Intro to Anthropology class almost late, scoping the room for an open desk as the prof set herself up at the pulpit. Pulled to sharp attention by an empty spot reflecting moonlight, though there was no window in the room—and certainly no moon tucked in the corner of the fluorescent-lit ceiling.


Sarah turned her head slightly to see that the light was emanating from a young woman sitting in the desk one over. Unambiguously inviting Sarah to step across a threshold that had nothing and everything to do with the forthcoming survey content of humanity’s entire becoming.


Sarah felt herself float down to the moonlit seat, not noticing that her hair was full of dried leaves from having spent the ten minutes before class buried under a pile of the most magnificently coloured fall foliage she had ever seen, in a courtyard on campus no one but her seemed to know about, turning her face to the source of the light.


“Hi, I’m Sarah, and you are glowing. What’s your name?”


To which the other young woman replied, “Ariadne.”


Sarah lit up brighter than a thousand stars, beaming a cosmic radiance that travelled through Ariadne’s eyes, right into her muted and jittery first-day-of-class heart. “Ariadne! Of course! I am so very pleased to meet you here on Naxos, where you’ve just awakened from a very bizarre dream!”


“You’re a strange one,” Ariadne responded playfully, emanating warmth like a midwinter hearth when the world outside is frozen. Moonlight drawing Sarah in, sunlight shining unimpeded in return.


Ariadne’s eyes danced specks of gold in a halo of earthy green akin to the yellow flame she plucked from Sarah’s tangles. Which Ariadne proceeded to place face down on the tiny surface of desk attached to the lecture hall seat, ripping a lined sheet from her wire-bound notebook with her left hand as she pulled a length of graphite from a bag in her pocket with her right, staining her long fingers silver as she disappeared the leaf beneath her paper, smirking as it reappeared in contour with the rapid rubbing of her hand. Her brow furrowed in serious concentration as she wrote a few lines underneath, handing the paper back 
to Sarah before slipping the leaf between the pages of her textbook.


Sarah felt a tingling aliveness travel from the page through all her pathways of sensation, illuminating filigrees like the most delicate leaf veins through the numbness blanketing her heart when she read what Ariadne had inscribed:














———



				Ariadne collected marvels, as Sarah once had. Picking up bits of the world’s magnificence to take home and draw at 
various angles, considering their formation, making gestures in response—moving Sarah to join in with her own creativities.


Together they gathered detritus sharpened to shared meaning. Each walk through the city an expedition! So that when Sarah was with Ariadne, a bus ride downtown felt as exciting as Alice falling down a rabbit hole, never knowing what might come.


To Sarah, Ariadne was the world’s living beauty personified. She drew eyes toward her like the moon draws and mystifies sunlight. Sarah privileged with the closest closeness.




———


With Trevor, the flame flickered haphazardly to fire. It took Sarah a while to know she was burning.


She noticed him first as a sort of after-flare slinking out from the shadows of the Student Union building to the coffee counter, almost always within moments of Ariadne departing.


Sarah could see he was orbiting Ariadne as she was. She could see he had no idea at all what he was orbiting. She could see he had a whole massive world inside him dying to break out, barely able to look Sarah in the eye as he ordered his coffee, as though forces were agitating words at intergalactic speeds that he could not bring himself to speak. How adorable, Sarah thought. And maybe a little frantic?


She could sense that Trevor had no idea how to live in his own body. That his body was a pulsing quasar, at the centre of which was a black hole that Trevor was seeking to fill to assuage a great hunger.




———


Once they became an official couple, Sarah liked to joke that they fell in love under the influences of Ariadne and Vlad the Impaler.


The medieval history class discussion Trevor had successfully manoeuvred into a digression regarding Vlad the Impaler and his relation to the modern figure of Dracula was the first time Sarah heard Trevor speak a full sentence. Upending her impression of him as an awkward kid who could barely talk when she served his Americanos “short.” Sometimes over-pouring just to get a reaction out of him. Watching the alarm flare as the water trickled just a tad over short to goad him. Stopping as soon as his eyebrows lifted, offering him slightly disgruntled relief.


Sarah would learn years later from Sean that he had roped Trevor into sitting in on a fourth-year “Monsters and Myths” film studies course with him the semester prior to this fateful discussion, and that Trevor had basically repeated verbatim the notes he’d taken down from the overhead slides about Vlad the Impaler as historical vs. Dracula as cult figure.


Sarah would neatly interpret this new information with matured recognition gained from experience that Trevor was not after all an unparalleled genius, but that he had, as 
all young scholars did, absorbed knowledge from the minds of those who preceded him, and that the important part was the unique way Trevor had made the knowledge his own, then used that knowledge to make sense of things in ways Sarah and the other students in the class had never thought of before.


In the beginning, Sarah couldn’t tell if she liked or hated Trevor. He said things in class that made her cringe, but so many other things that sparked her curiosity. Her uneasiness grew to excitement over the course of the semester as she found herself transitioning from slight repulsion at his sometimes obnoxious proclamations to a state of fairly insistent daydreaming about how to continue the conversation with him once class ended, imagining all sorts of scenarios for how to get him to pay attention to her.




———


He was paying attention. Very close attention. Studying Sarah.


Trevor knew Sarah as the girl who was always in the company of the one he’d been captivated by at the open mic, the one who drew 
all
 of his attention.


Sarah being the reason he’d signed up for the class in the first place. Because he’d overheard her in the café telling a co-worker she had signed up for medieval history class—“a crush on the professor,” how inane—which had prompted Trevor to dial into the course registration system as soon as he got home, swapping out the Chaucer class he was only minorly interested in—a literary necessity he could make 
up for next semester—in favour of medieval history, convinced this bubbly wild-haired brunette had just dropped him the clue that would guide him down the pathway to his soulmate.




———


“Hi, I’m Sarah,” she said when Trevor sat down beside her a few weeks after the start of their sophomore year.


Not wanting to seem too eager, Trevor had taken those weeks to hop around from desk to desk, each day choosing a different location, so it would seem to anyone looking that he had no particular reason for sitting here or there, taking note that Sarah always sat in the same place. Finally, on his chosen day, he made sure to arrive right after Sarah, so he could get the seat next to hers—three rows back, four in from the centre aisle on the left.


“Troy, er Trevor,” Trevor responded, ducking his head, wincing, then a drawn-out pause, his face doing all sorts of weird things. “You’re friends with…the most beautiful girl on campus?”


“Ariadne? Yes, we’re roommates.”


At which point he looked at Sarah with the most intense look she’d possibly ever seen. A look that lasted about two seconds, before he quickly looked away.


She felt something within her gape open.




———



				Compulsion mixed with confounding unease. He fiercely wanted something of her. Something that had nothing to 
do with her, and yet, she felt herself wanting to find some way of giving it to him.


It was completely disorienting.




———


It was not the first time she’d had some version of this conversation. Boys approached her often to ask about her beautiful friend—too intimidating to approach directly.


Sarah made a game of tracking the facial expressions of strangers when she was out with Ariadne. Men stumbled, bumped into things, dropped their sentences, following her with their eyes, as though she were a disturbance in space-time.


Ariadne had learned to put up horse blinders, carrying herself in the world cut off, protecting herself from the allure she inspired.


Glances fell on Sarah as an afterthought, overlooking her as though she were not even there. It gave Sarah a certain power. Being inside the force gave her a certain vantage to observe, while bestowing Ariadne with mooring. Sarah was Ariadne’s anchor. She dropped others’ desires back to earth, freeing Ariadne of them so she did not have to be alone in a world in which everyone wanted to eat her up.


With Trevor, Sarah felt implicated somehow. Unanchored. She needed to get away from him.




———


But then Trevor had started up about Vlad. And Sarah was rapt.


Such surprising things to say!


In this manner, through the alchemy of Sarah’s curiosity and Trevor’s keen interest in the things that interested him, they developed a sort of friendship.


Sarah offered insights, feeling sparked, because what she offered set him off, she could feel this. Her spark enlivening his.


They enjoyed one another’s minds.


And Sarah was happy to hand out small clues when Trevor asked about the mystical Ariadne. Who he kept enough distance from that it might seem like he wasn’t following her, except that he clearly was. Which made him even more compelling. A strange smart kid with beautiful lips and secrets he kept to himself! Secrets Sarah might discover if she played Trevor’s game right.




———


A few weeks before the end of class, Sarah and Ariadne had a conversation that went like this:


“You know the weirdly bright guy from my medieval history class I was telling you about? Trevor? He’s totally infatuated with you.”


“Which guy?”


“The one I told you about! Remember? Weirdly into Vlad the Impaler, but in like, a pop culture meets history kind of way that is sort of sexy? Hangs out with the beautiful tall blond guy—the only other person on campus in the same category of gorgeousness as you, so you might have taken notice.”


“Hush, Sarah, stop. What do you want me to say? So, this guy you like that has never so much as even talked to me is infatuated with me?”


“I didn’t say I liked him, Ariadne!”


“Of course you do! I can see the little smile on your lips. You like him, just admit it. You just said he was sexy! It’s okay to like someone, Sarah.”


“But he likes you!”


“He doesn’t even know me. I have Absolutely No Interest! 
None at all
. You know I have eyes only for you, because you so desperately delight me with your quirky weirdness that somehow is a perfect match for my own.”


And Sarah had blushed. Blood rushing inside a little. Or a lot. Confused by what she was feeling, never quite knowing what Ariadne meant when she said things like this. Because Sarah felt the same way, loving every single moment of time in Ariadne’s company, in complete adoration, sometimes feeling a pull that sparked with a kind of hotter energy, which always dissolved into giggles.


“He’s funny, Ariadne! And super smart. Almost as smart as you.”


“Still, no interest. Especially not if he’s infatuated with me before he has ever even spoken one word to me. Infatuation, if you hadn’t noticed, leaves little room for the object to articulate their own subjectivity. Plus, I don’t have time for that stuff, Sarah. My mind is so full of other things.”


“What, like you’d rather pretend-kiss Captain Picard on the TV screen than a real guy?”


“Yup, any day. Captain Picard is a total babe. And he’s not obsessed with me. He’s got other things on his mind.”


“It’s true, he does have a lot of things on his mind. All the nuance of how to interpret the Prime Directive! How to respond to hostile life forms with tact! How to keep Troi and Riker from ripping each other’s clothes off in the heat of battle! I want to pretend-kiss him too. But I was looking at Trevor’s lips the other day. Watching the little bit of spittle gather at the corners of his mouth when he gets excited about something—the way his tongue slips over and licks it up, poking out of the corner of his lips, and I couldn’t help it, my lips got all tingly, and I wanted to kiss him. Bizarre, right? I’m such a weirdo. Like he’s ever going to be into me!”


“Sarah, it’s not bizarre. I mean, it is to me, because like I said, zero interest, and the spittle part is kind of gross, but it’s cute too. It’s super cute. It’s okay to feel that. To feel curious and excited about someone! I’m happy for you. Just hang out with him. You’re wonderfully bizarre. But that’s why I love you! You’re amazing! He’ll see you if you give him a chance. He’d be stupid not to. And if he can’t get past his obsession with the not-me-he-thinks-is-me and see you, then he’s a chump. And you’ve got me, and Picard, and Troi, and Riker. And any number of people you haven’t met yet whose lips will also cause yours to tingle.”


Sarah had never seen Ariadne show any interest in boys. Or girls for that matter. Any interest in anyone, really, other than Sarah, and Picard, and what went on inside herself. She 
always had her head in a book. Or her mind was drifted out to whatever she was creating or observing—her drawings, the clouds, or rocks—wholly absorbed in whatever she saw there.


Sarah knew why. Ariadne, in one of her more open moments, had told her, “Sarah, when my mom died my heart closed up. I felt like I was walking in the world in a body detached from the life that had once animated it. Trapped inside myself. Surrounded by people who felt as far off as the closest galaxy. Swirling masses of energies I wanted only to shrink away from. And then I met you. And the first time you smiled at me with a head tangled in flaming leaves, I knew I would love you forever. That I could trust you, that you were made of the same stuff as me. You laugh me back to life, Sarah. And you surprise me. I love sharing my life with you.”




———



				It was a kind of love that needed Sarah to neither do nor be anything that she wasn’t already. Sarah did not have to make anything of herself to hold Ariadne’s attention. She was not driven toward gaining Ariadne’s interest as she was toward gaining Trevor’s. With Ariadne it was like zooming around in space together, with Trevor it was like she was in a car driving somewhere with all these sideroads she felt compelled to follow. As though she was being led astray, and she wanted this more than anything—fear intermingled with desire. Because what might she find there in the darkness if she cut the headlights? What parts of 
herself might these tracks lead to that she could not find on her own? That Ariadne would never lead her to, because Ariadne’s love was so full of celestial light that it illuminated everything.


Ariadne loved, but did not want to possess or be possessed by Sarah. She had lived through enough variations of tragedy in her millennia-long myth to be a bit done with all that nonsense.


Sarah sometimes wanted to break into Ariadne’s dreams and dream them for herself. It was ferocious, but Sarah felt it was beautiful. 
Find yourself
, Ariadne sometimes had to remind Sarah. 
Stop looking for me
.




———


Sarah thought Ariadne told her everything, as she told Ariadne everything. Full and total disclosure so you were ready for danger together when it inevitably arrived—Ariadne, her living, breathing confidante. No one left out in the cold. She thought their total companionship explicit.


Until the night Ariadne did not come home.


Casting Sarah into painful, confusing bewilderment. Blanketing her in acute terror with waking dreams of Ariadne standing in for every maimed female body the media had gifted her with seeing, degraded by monsters lurking in shadows, her good spirit sucked out of her eyes by gaping mouths that laughed and taunted as Sarah scrambled to save her, failing over and over again. At 3 a.m. finally falling into a fitfull sleep.


She awoke before daybreak to find that Ariadne was still gone, wracking her mind for any clue she might have missed for where Ariadne might be. Resolving to head to campus after reporting her missing—Ariadne’s last known whereabouts.


The police operator offered no solace, just a deeper despair, Sarah mad with certainty, talked down to like an unknowing child. Told not enough time had elapsed to report a missing person. She tried to make them understand the danger of Ariadne’s beauty, how someone must have taken her, that they needed to start looking right away! Convinced beyond all reason that Ariadne lay somewhere ravished and abandoned, isolated and left for dead.


Sarah was the only one who could find her!


She paced room to room, frantic. Feral in the cage of her own mind, cycling through starts and stops and impossibilities—
nowhere, nowhere! Ariadne has been sucked into the dark abyss forever!
 “No, think. Think. You can find her. She’ll come back. Where can she be? Slow down, it’s there, you just have to”—stopped in her tracks, darkness making a mirror of the inside world at the dining room window—glimpsing herself looking just as her mother did each time her father disappeared.


The same wild eyes. The same desperate searching.


Hope washed out of her that with daylight would come deliverance—her mother’s voice on the other end of the line, coaxing her back to her senses.


Sarah collapsed. Madness to mush. Floor on her hands, feeling into the hardwood. Here. Now. She could not drag her mother into the same hell her father had dragged her into so many times.


Ariadne didn’t have any friends in the city besides her.


She would not call Ariadne’s father until she knew something for certain.


Her sister was too far away, unreachable.


There was no one to call.


Sarah retreated to a silent place beyond time.


Until Ariadne whisked through the door at 9:36 a.m., at which point Sarah pounced, embracing her tightly, smoothing down her hair, kissing her cheeks. “Are you okay, Ariadne? What happened?”


Ariadne met Sarah’s frenzied gaze with eyes full of pleasurable excitement—pupils wider than possible, as though possessed.


Sarah pulled back, terrified. “What?”


“Sweetie, I’m okay, I just went out to a party with some of the folks from my art class.”


“Why do your eyes look like that? What did they do to you?” Sarah demanded.


“No one did anything to me, Sarah,” Ariadne laughed. “I guess my pupils are still dilated from the E. It was weird, everyone looked so wide open. When I saw my eyes in the mirror, I burst out laughing at how hard they were working to let in as much light as possible, like they were hungry for light.”


“E?”


“Ecstasy—you know.”


“You did E, on your own? Without me? With people I don’t even know?”


“Sarah, I’m allowed to have a life and experiences of my own! Just like you need to have a life and experiences of your own so it’s more fun to share with each other! I’d love to do E with you sometime, to have you meet my friends. If you want to.”


“I don’t want to!” Sarah snapped. Crestfallen, backing out of the room. “I never want to,” she whispered as she rushed herself to her bedroom to hide beneath her blankets, shaking, her whole world closing down around her. The terror she had felt through the night cascading, filling her mind with strange corrupt stories that contradicted all goodness. Her mind trying to save itself by severing the bond to make the terror stop.


Ariadne was not truly her friend, after all. Not if she was going to lead her to such darkness and abandon her there.




———



				Ariadne slept all that day while Sarah made a petulant show of doing the things that needed doing around the house, smashing pots, bumping the vacuum into every possible obstacle, running laundry in the room right next to Ariadne’s so the noise would reverberate through the walls, growing her self-loathing with every clang and rumble, finally exhausting herself with punishment she was visiting only on herself, walking to the beach where the ocean absorbed her 
futility, lapping undulating truths at her in crests and waves.


Perhaps it was not the end of their friendship? Perhaps Ariadne had not meant to hurt her? Perhaps she was not uncaring and Sarah was truly special to her and her new friends would not steal her away if Sarah could somehow manage not to ruin it all?


In the evening, Sarah returned home frazzled, but less mean, resolved to regain her friend. When she walked in Ariadne smiled at her with such easy happiness, tucked into the breakfast nook with a pot of tea and a book.


“You’re finally home! Thank you for cleaning. It looks great. Come have tea with me and tell me where you’ve been all day.”


And just like that, the wall came down. How could she bear this kindness after what she’d just been through that Ariadne could not even feel?


“I can’t, Ariadne, I have too much schoolwork,” Sarah replied bitterly, refusing to make eye contact with her. This stranger in her house.


“Sarah, don’t do that to me.”


“Do what?”


“Shut me out like that because you’re hurt.”


“I’m not! I just don’t want to hang out tonight. I have a life too!”


“You are shutting me out. And you are hurt, I can feel it. You’re all tight and balled up, like you’re going to explode. But don’t do that to me. I didn’t do anything wrong. It’s okay to be hurt, but it’s not okay to take it out on me. I love you, 
you’re my dearest, but you don’t get to choose what I do or be angry with me after because I did something without you.”


Sarah could feel herself getting small, shrinking, trying to pull her outsides into her insides to fill up the swirling space.


“I’m sorry I hurt you,” Ariadne went on. “I didn’t mean to, but I can see that I did. It was thoughtless of me not to call. I guess I was worried you might try to talk me out of it, and I wanted to try it, so I didn’t.”


Sarah touched her hand to her heart. It was hurting so badly, cracking apart, it felt like all her breath had gone out of her and she would never breathe again. She was losing Ariadne; she could feel it. She was awful. Ariadne thought she would hold her back from the life she wanted, and now Ariadne was casting her out. All the goodness they had shared was slipping away because now Ariadne could see what a frightful jealous scared awful creature Sarah was, not worthy of her love after all.


“I was terrified, Ariadne. Terrified. I thought all sorts of terrible things…my dad…”


“Sarah. Oh. I’m so sorry. I’m right here,” Ariadne said, approaching her, placing her hand over Sarah’s. “Right here, Sarah, come back.”


Sarah recoiled, bumping against the stove, but then she looked up and met Ariadne’s eyes and they were so clear. Seeing her. Loving her, feeling alongside her, instead of casting her out. She softened, moved her hands to Ariadne’s, then tried to project her pain into Ariadne, so that Ariadne 
might feel the depth of her anguish, washing Sarah clean with Ariadne’s goodness instead.


“No Sarah, not like that. Stay here in the room with me. Feel my hand on yours, your hand on your heart, we’re here, two people, together in our kitchen. Breathe, baby. It’s you, and me, here now. You can feel your way through these really hard things, and come back. I’m here beside you. Still me, and I love you.”

Sometimes it seems all there is.


I find myself engulfed in a seemingly impossible fog through which I cannot find my way, the whole world indistinct. I hear voices, perceive shapes, but they all seem empty, meaningless. Sarah Chooses


To enter into Sarah and Ariadne’s house, one had to dodge between hanging stalactite fabric structures in the small boot room; like an entrance to a cave, or womb, depending on what time of day it was and how the light shone through the fabrics.


The two young women had been inspired by an archaeology course on human dwellings and an art history course on interior design and gendered constructions of family. For their final project for the art history course, they had come up with a manifesto describing their home as a living installation, the goal of which was to skew expectations of contemporary human dwelling using bricolage that combined natural and fabricated elements to cultivate space for 
existential uncertainty, bewilderment, soul-wrenching sadness, and love.


Throughout the course of the semester they had documented their process of transforming an otherwise mundane mid-century bungalow geared toward the postwar nuclear family into a living installation, papering over one of the walls in the living room with a side table of art supplies at the ready for spontaneous acts of artistry. Visitors were encouraged to participate by adding marks to the tableau already there. Every month the roommates papered over the existing scene with blank paper so the wall came to resemble an absurdist version of a centuries-old farmhouse layered in successive applications of wallpaper. Or what they described as an archive of concealment and possible experience of living space attesting to a fascination with whimsy. An intrusion into the status quo.


One night, while high on mushrooms, Ariadne and Sarah found themselves painting the living room wall and their own bodies various hues of green. Sarah was not comfortable with the idea of doing mind-altering drugs made in a laboratory with strangers, but mushrooms were living organisms that imbued mystical potencies, so she had asked Ariadne to take them with her in the safety of their own home.


Under their influence, the wall was taking on a sort of abstract expressionist flavour, deeply reminiscent of early Helen Frankenthaler, until Sarah started detailing in networks of interlocking filaments in black ink that, no surprise, given the psilocybin influence, resembled mycelial pathways.


“Whoa, Sarah! Don’t you think that looks like the diagram of the electrochemical signals in the brain Lucy showed us in her biochem textbook?”


At which point they stopped their painting, deciding that the very best thing to do would be to lie flat on their backs and watch their hallucinations of a forest in communication play out before their eyes, holding hands and giggling, and then, at some point, turning toward one another, eyes understanding that what flowed between them was pulling them into the unfolding world they had painted. Pulling them into one another, as close as they could become, like hyphae growing to know one another by touch.

It is not Trevor’s story

It is Sarah and Ariadne’s

Sarah could see her role so clearly in Trevor’s dreams. They were her dreams too, after all.

Later, reading other versions, sometimes she would be.

Tell Me Your Dreams


“Tell me your dreams,” Sarah whispered, every morning—gazing at Trevor as though he might disappear into the invisible world inside his mind, as he did every night when he closed his eyes. This invisible world Sarah felt in every nerve of her body. In the bathroom afterward, making sure to pee so she wouldn’t get a UTI, she would find her eyes in the mirror, watching her face morph, becoming obscure, almost as though when it was all stripped away there was no real face at all—just a field of ever-arising sensations her mind had so much trouble making sense of. Trevor there, filling up her inner space, so she felt as much Trevor as Sarah. A screensaver of her devotion.


Then she would wash her hands, rinse her face in cold water, feeling water’s uncomplicated truth before returning to bed, where she would look at Trevor in the obscured orange light from the street lamp outside, seeping through the thin bedsheet he had pinned over the window as a curtain, seeing his wholeness, feeling herself fall towards sleep. “Trevor’s Sarah,” she would whisper, guiding herself to dream back in her place beside him.

Your Moon-eyed girl












The Proposal


Trevor agreed to marry Sarah because he didn’t want to lose her. She was, as his father had put it, “the kind of woman you marry if you’re lucky enough to find her.”


A bit of fatherly advice Trevor took seriously, because it had been one of the rare exchanges between him and his father that felt like it had anything of substance to it—Walt sharing something that seemed to come from within.


“I just don’t know if I want to, Pop. I don’t understand why we have to change things. They feel good the way they are.”


“A man has to grow up sometime,” Walt said. “I know you and I don’t always see eye to eye on how a man should live his life, but I’ll tell you one thing I know: Sarah’s a good woman. She takes care of you. She’s stuck by you through these years you’ve lived apart. She’s a hard worker. She’ll be a good mother. And she’s funny. You laugh together. She’s not one to let go of, Trev. And a man doesn’t have to stop living either, once he’s settled down. There are many ways to make a marriage.”


“What do you mean by that?”


“There are things that can be figured out, Trev, said or unsaid, and while it may look to you like me and your mom live boring lives, there’s a lot you don’t know about what happens because you’re our kid, and you don’t need to. You don’t stop being a man because you get married.”


A statement like this coming from Walt? It had been a shock to Trevor. There was more than one way to make a marriage? His parents’ marriage seemed more like a business arrangement for child-rearing in the most removed, orderly way possible than a life together. It was almost impossible for Trevor to believe there was anything beneath the surface of the façade. But Trevor could not deny the sense his father had made of things, either. Sarah was a hard worker. She’d offered to work while he went on to do his Ph.D., and then after he was done, they could have babies, and she would take time off to be with them. She was practical. And generous. She was offering him the time and freedom he needed to not worry about finances while he 
studied. His parents would help, but with Sarah’s contributions, he wouldn’t have to worry about anything. She would take care of him. Granting him one kind of freedom at the expense of another.




———


On his twenty-fifth birthday, Trevor found a note in Sarah’s handwriting on his pillow, accompanied by a hand-drawn map leading him to a slip of beach along the seawall in Stanley Park. “Strange,” he thought. Sarah was in Southern Manitoba doing research for a piece on tornadoes. Maybe she’d mailed it to one of his roommates and had them help her out with a surprise? It would be just like her to come up with an elaborate romantic plan he could never come up with an equal to.


He found her there just before sunset, sitting on a boulder.


She smiled, patting the spot beside her.


“I don’t think you’re going to get here on your own, Trevor, but this is important to me. I need to know where I’m going from here. So here goes! Will you marry me, Trevor Hart?” she asked, holding out a small blue velvet box, inside of which was the tiniest book Trevor had ever seen.


The book was wrapped in a piece of parchment that read: 
One hundred pages for the one hundred years we might live together.
 It was so delicate; hand-stitched and bound within a birchbark cover. All of the pages blank. Inside the box was also a strike-anywhere match, taped to a note on a piece of flash card. The note read: 
This is it. Here we are. Say yes and we begin filling the pages of the book with whatever 
we make of our life together; or light the match and burn the book, and from here we go our separate ways.


Trevor looked at Sarah, looked deep into her brown eyes. He did not want to see Sarah go up in flames. These were eyes that tethered him to the world, made him feel like he might have a place in it, after all. Eyes accompanied by strangenesses such as this, things he could never expect. His Sarah.


And yet. Only this? Only this for the rest of time?


They were so young!


Go forward in time together, or each their separate way? Why did it have to be a choice? Why did Sarah have to force this on him? He wanted to do neither. He wanted to throw the book and the match in the water, some other choice outside of this one she was cornering him into.


He picked up the match, ran it against the rough boulder, igniting it to flame, holding the little book in his other hand.


The flame flickered in the wind as he moved it toward the book, looking Sarah in the eyes, watching her hope falter.


And then, at the last moment, he blew it out, inhaled, and on the next exhale replied, “Yes, Sarah Hart, I will.”


“You’re cruel, Trevor, you know that?” she said, punching his arm, mock-hurting him in return. And then, “Sarah Hart?” she laughed. “You want me to change my name?”


“Will you,” he asked, “change your name for me?”


If he was giving up so much, she could give something up too.
