Chapter Three


Dream Come True




The Labyrinth


They entered the labyrinth.


Sarah holding the ball of thread; passing it to Trevor as they made their way in, Trevor blindfolded, Sarah leading the way.


Trevor unravelling it.


Sarah assuring Trevor he was the hero of her story.


Purifying past errors, forging a path for her co-starring role in stunning red velvet tights and a gorgeous white silk tunic.


When they reached the centre of the labyrinth they paused, Sarah removed the blindfold, and they looked at one another with glints in their eyes, enacting time.


There was no Minotaur lurking in the shadows.


It was a tourist hedge maze in Bavaria! No need for the ball of thread, or the fear, after all.


Only one direction to follow; the signs clearly read in multiple languages: 
No going back!


Trevor dropped the ball.


Scooping Sarah up in his arms, lifting her to him.


“I’m going to fuck you now,” he whispered in her ear.


“Yes,” she affirmed, expectant.


He turned her around and pulled her leggings down. “So much better than slaying a Minotaur,” he said, wildly pleasing her—the quick thrill of being caught, murmurs and footsteps of the next round of tourists, a few turns of the labyrinth behind them.


Rushing their clothes back up, linking hands to run the rest of the way before being seen.


A path clearly marked. No chance of getting lost.


Ball of thread forgotten.




———


Emerging from the labyrinth, they saw two humungous black swans floating, as though props.


“Black swans?” Trevor asked.


“Are they real?” Sarah murmured.


“I don’t know.”


“They are,” Sarah exclaimed, “look!”




———


How fantastic to be invited into the myth unfolding around them.


First the labyrinth, now the black swans! They really were making life their own.


They wished to continue the story—and as humans are fond of mimetic gestures to affirm shared meaning, they did what they could with their own bodies to become swan-like, lifting their arms above their heads in mock-swan necks, fingertip beaks touching as they brought their lips together to seal the future in place.


Their true beginning.




———


In the midst of the kiss, Trevor suddenly remembered a class trip he’d all but forgotten, to a wildlife refuge where mute, trumpeter, and tundra swans floated gracefully on placid waters.


The mute swans were introduced. Invasive. Bigger and more aggressive than the native tundras and trumpeters. Orange beaks with black crests. These were the kind of swans that showed up in the picture books at school. The tundra swans were the smallest, black-beaked like the trumpeters, but quieter, and only present during their spring migration. It was hard to tell from a distance. Trevor had remembered these details for the test but forgotten them when looking at the swans on the lake. They were swans, plain and simple. White swans with pretty necks swimming around on water. Easy targets to throw rocks at.


He hadn’t been trying to hit her head. Just her big fluffy white back. He thought the rock would bounce off and 
splash into the water and the bird wouldn’t even feel it. Maybe make a loud panicked ruckus, flapping and scattering complaining cygnets in surprise.


There had been a ruckus. The cygnets did flee.


And the mother had keeled over, but only after letting out a shriek that momentarily froze Trevor in time, an eternity of seconds, before he tried to run away.


At which point he had tripped on his own feet, hitting his head hard on the gravel walkway while his classmates looked on in shock.




———


Trevor flinched—away from the kiss, pulling his arms swiftly to his chest, ducking his head down.


“Are you okay?” Sarah asked. “What is it?”


“Nothing, sorry! A weird shock in my arm, like hitting a funny bone.”

Trevor hadn’t known which was the mom and which the dad! He hadn’t thought about it. He hadn’t believed his desire to throw the rock at the bird on the water and killing mama swan could go together—that one might be the outcome of the action he had undertaken without thinking through the consequences. He was eight years old, such a little kid. No nature sanctuaries for him after that. No other class trips either.

Sarah, too, was at the behest of a swan history secretly roused from the depths of memory by the kiss.


A sudden image of a book she had made for imaginary Ariadne as a child. 
Swan Variations
, a title she had picked up from listening to Glenn Gould’s 
Goldberg Variations
 with her father on his old record player—the soundtrack to her bookmaking.


How could she have forgotten? There had been black swans, and purple ones, rainbow swans, swans that were part turtle and maybe even had human hair. Swans with clothing, also—very intricate dresses and pinafores. Swans in regular workaday clothes—overalls and rubber boots, flower-print aprons, or power suits with exceedingly large shoulder pads, which they didn’t really need with their large wings. But she was a child of the eighties in love with the Bette Midler and Lily Tomlin movie 
Big Business
, and took her cues from them about what kind of clothing could make a swan woman successful.












Sarah had only seen the movie once at a neighbour’s house, because they did not have a TV set on the homestead, but it had left an impression.


Enough of an impression that in their sophomore and junior years of university, Sarah and real-life Ariadne had dressed up as the switched twins, and Sarah got to be the Bette characters both times. Ariadne could in no way pass for Lily Tomlin, but that didn’t matter since no one had guessed who they were supposed to be anyway. Which suited the duo just fine.


The memory made Sarah’s lips pull back slightly in a smile as she and Trevor kissed. Perhaps causing the shock to run through him, she feared, as he retracted his arms, shielding himself from her memory surge.


“I have to be careful what I think about,” Sarah thought. And then, “How awful of me! Right in the middle of our kiss! Poor Trevor.”




———


Barely remembered successful swan women and dead swan moms aside, Sarah and Trevor were on their honeymoon! Sarah took Trevor’s hand to reassure him, offering a smile he graciously returned.


“I’m hungry,” Trevor said.


“Starving!” Sarah echoed.


“Excuse me,” they heard in German-accented English from a man approaching with a very large camera, offering 
to post them a snapshot of their forever-after kiss candidly captured, in exchange for permission to use it in his portfolio.


“Astonishing!” Sarah exclaimed, picturing it hanging on the wall in the castle they would make together. Seeing it so very, very clearly, as though she herself had been the candid photographer framing the moment in time.


The Making of a Domestic Goddess


“You, Sarah, are my Domestic Goddess,” Trevor would often say to her in their castle in Mississauga, during the dream-come-true phase of playing house together—secured thanks to an auspicious inheritance from Trevor’s maternal grandmother when Trevor landed the teaching position that, in concert with the forthcoming publication of his first book, put him right on track for future aspirations of tenure.


“This is my husband, we’re newlyweds,” Sarah would say as often as possible—for what? A year? A little less? A little longer? Maybe three?—when introducing him. How long could being newlywed last? Could Sarah say it for a decade if it felt that way? Sometimes she thought she might. Imagine if she still said it when they were celebrating their golden anniversary! She thought she would! She dreamed from now until then, one golden line, and the whole fifty years they would still be newlyweds! That’s how perfect their marriage was. How romantic if they even had it engraved on their matching headstones for their eternal life together—
Newlyweds, Forever.
 My god, 
she was so delighted! She would be having newlywed sex all her life!




———



				Sarah was keeping husband and home and Trevor was so very pleased to have a wife he could find whenever he wished, somewhere in the house doing the mysterious things wives did while husbands did other things. He would look up from his work and think, “I have a wife, I should go and find her,” and there she would be, looking up from her wifely work to offer him the most welcoming smile. And with this smile he would be drawn in, magnetized, sidling up to her in the kitchen as she deftly handled a knife, gently, gently, to make sure she did not cut a lovely finger, sliding the knife out of her hand to turn her toward him into his arms, looking straight in her eyes and kissing her once, swiftly, on the tip of the nose, then spinning her back, gliding the knife back into her palm, sidling away, sashaying out the door, back toward his study, from where he had emerged, just momentarily, to let his wife know just how very grateful he was that she was making dinner for him while he worked. Just a swift kiss, but also a promise. A promise that later, there would be more. More to the so-much there was already as Sarah sipped her glass of chilled Sauvignon, spatchcocking a free-range chicken and rubbing it down with Himalayan salt while Bonnie Prince Billy crooned from the turntable, alive in the creative solitude of her preparations as Trevor made magic in the creative solitude of his study. His study! His very own study where he would create his great works. 
Breaking once the chicken was crisp and succulent to savour Sarah’s dedications—another glass of wine for Sarah and spritzer for Trevor, some Sharon Jones & the Dap-Kings to see them through the meal, onto the couch, layers peeled, a swift record swap—Depeche Mode’s “I Feel You”—to reveal the naked stuff underneath, up against the doorjamb, and then, the promise fulfilled, the performance sealed with Trevor’s standing O.


A pause, a brief rest on the living room floor, some quick cleanup of spilled fluids with a T-shirt from the laundry hamper, Trevor beginning to gather his clothes for the return to work, and then, a requested encore by Sarah—she too desiring an O for her performance. Not standing, but reclined on her up-cycled sidewalk sofa reupholstered in vintage sea blue and chartreuse chintz found for five dollars at an estate sale, her back arched graceful and long, hips lifted skyward, meeting Trevor’s mouth, tongue reaching toward and toward and toward her as he held one supportive palm underneath, guiding the arrhythmic fingers of his other hand within—Sarah’s cushioned neck a resplendent S, her head haloed in the welcoming plush of her contrasting black-and-white-chevron couch pillow.


Yes, newlywed sex was where it was at! Such promise the kiss and the future held. How far they had come together from their first awkward fumbling.


Promise fulfilled, Trevor headed back to his study for the rest of the night and Sarah tidied up the kitchen, a teeny bit miffed. Just a teeny bit, that Trevor had opted out—but 
he had so much to do, she knew, and though she had an article she was working on for a travel magazine about Bavarian castles, she could work angles in her mind as she scrubbed chicken grease off the cooking pot, possessed of the glorious gift of multitasking. Images and words linked up in a kind of interwoven sequence as she prepared a pot of tea, sipped water to speed alcohol metabolism, snapped Tupperware lids into place, arranged Trevor’s lunch portion and hers separately in the fridge for the next day, wiped the counters and backsplash, and when it was all tidied up, all put away, ready for tomorrow’s promises, Sarah, with a mind now clear and still floating on the rippling waves of fulfilled arousal, could confidently sit at her lovely little desk in the bedroom and write a few hundred words.


Feeling for all the world in that moment like a woman living a dream.


Domestic Goddess
—a jibe between them. One of those marital binders that ridiculed convention while also upholding it, melding a couple into a shared articulation of fidelity, indicating that they both played a part in something simultaneously exclusive, homey, divine, and ever-enduring—the heterosexual monogamous pair-bond of the educated modern urban couple.


This endearment had been birthed into existence on Trevor’s thirty-second birthday, when he had arrived home from a ten-hour day at the university after having taught an undergrad Intro to Creative Writing course, led a graduate fiction workshop, attended a departmental meeting, 
held office hours attended by one disgruntled, one besotted, and two anxious students, hosted one-to-one conferences with two grad students from his workshop, and edited a conceptual prose poem sequence on Deleuze’s immanent ethics featuring a man with four arms and no feet turning around on his hands in endless repetitive circuits, wearing a hole in the carpet and the skin on his hands.


The poem had been accepted by a reputable literary journal, and Trevor’s revisions were due. He had gone over and over his final line on his bike ride home, trying to decide between 
castrated
, 
gelded
, and 
desexed
 as the final word, to find that Sarah had rearranged the living room, added new décor, and organized a surprise five-course 
cuisine française
 formal dinner party that was to be attended by a few of Trevor’s colleagues and their respective significant others, who were due to arrive at six o’clock for aperitifs.


It was 5:35. Trevor had a short twenty-five minutes to get ready, eight of which were to be spent with Sarah giving him a timed blow job on the couch, the next twelve having a quick shower and then slipping into the suit Sarah had laid out for him on the bed, arriving in good form, with five minutes to spare, to pour the pastis and welcome his dinner guests.


Sarah informed him of his itinerary as he walked in the door, hands already on his zipper as the word 
castrated
 still swirled somewhere behind his eyes, whizzing out his right earhole as one of her hands cupped him from underneath while the other stroked him until he hardened, at which 
point her lips spread, like an octopus sucker on a tasty fish, suctioning around his tip.


As Sarah delivered the happy-birthday 
Welcome Home!
 Trevor wished would happen after every exacting day at the office, he had a stimulating time taking in the living room redesign, complete with two new shelves Sarah had mounted.


One of the shelves displayed a select grouping of Trevor’s favourite books, and the other a select grouping of Sarah’s, all of which, Trevor noted with approval, had been gifts to her from him. Upon closer inspection, Trevor saw that the mounting braces were handcrafted wrought-iron swans, graceful necks bent upward so their beaks held up thick slabs of birch with strips of bark still crusted on the edges.


After emerging from the shower limp, limber, and steaming, Trevor found his 
habit
, as Sarah had called it, laid out atop a quilt, clearly made by Sarah, whose prominent feature was a pair of appliquéd black swans forming a heart in the centre as their beaks met in a kiss in front of a stitched replica of Rosenau Palace. One of the swans was stitched together from a collection of old Metallica T-shirts Trevor momentarily feared were his own, realizing upon closer inspection that they were blessedly sound of fabric and therefore not the beloved garments worn thin over time by his own sweat and heat. The other swan made from the crocheted dress Sarah had worn on their first date.


The quilt made of the bed almost a mirror reflecting the photograph on their bedroom wall, taken on their honeymoon, the two of them kissing, their heads encased within the heart made by their arms arched overhead, fingers meeting in the heart’s valley while the black swans floated idly in the misty background out of which the castle loomed—silly, slightly awkward, romantic and precious, just like the two of them.














The meal was served on the new dinnerware set that Sarah had painstakingly crafted at her twice-weekly evening community ceramics class—white plates with transfers of hand-sketched black swans. This time, instead of kissing, each piece in the collection demonstrated its own vignette of the swans living out their swan lives: slurping pond grasses, making nests, playing cards, reading books about pond maintenance. It was over-the-top ridiculous, thought Trevor, but touching too. So touching. The way Sarah was over-the-top-ridiculous and touching, about most things in life.


She needed to be kept busy, and she was between jobs, writing freelance now that Trevor was gainfully employed, taking the opportunity to really focus on her work and acquire new skill sets. Hence the ceramic swan plates, which, she had informed Trevor, were useful, sentimental, quirkily romantic, and serving her journalistic aspirations, since she’d been so inspired by the artisans in her class that she intended to write a piece about emergent crafting entrepreneurship.


“It’ll be tax deductible!” she informed Trevor after he followed her into the kitchen, carrying the cleared amuse-bouche plates, holding one up to really look at the design, wiping up a slick of duck liver pâté from a swan’s back and licking it off his finger.


“Babe, the plates are gorgeous, truly, and the shelves too. And the quilt! Sarah! You’ve really outdone yourself here. I mean, it’s over the top. Really. Over the fucking top. I’m honoured. But how much did this all cost?”


It felt like it echoed in her mind for seven sentences beyond, some part of her frozen, another wanting to reassure Trevor it was all good, all good, all of it good, and just for him: “Tax deductible, deductible de-duck-table, de-duuuck-taaaable, de-duh-duh-duh-duh-duck-table, tuh-ble, tuuuh-bull.”


And Trevor, looking at her with a strange mixture of confusion and awe as the echo filled her internal mind space, swelling tears up into her eyes, blessedly read something in her features that made him shift from the borderlands of domestic criticism back to the safe terrain of domestic 
kindness that averted rather than ignited an impending dinner-party dispute. After all she’d gone to! For him. All of this! It was crazy to think of all she’d planned out so far ahead of time. All the hours she’d put in for this grandiose spectacle for her husband’s thirty-second birthday!


He leaned toward her, pulling her close, taking back his words as best as words can possibly be taken back after they’ve been said and heard, existing now in that sensitive realm where they might yet be quickly forgiven rather than stored up in that special pantry in the mind in which spousal criticisms are kept, threatening to spill out in choreographed routines as each partner plays their part in fanning the flames of small hurts that might, over time, become deeply held resentments if left to linger unaddressed.


This time, Trevor extinguished rather than fanned, recognizing the gift for what it was, after all.


“Sarah, my dear sweet. You’re amazing. Truly. My wife, my swan, my quirky Domestic Goddess.”


He whispered this in her ear, hand on the back of her neck, his crotch pressing her ass into the kitchen counter lip behind her, slipping his hand up her thigh under her black silk dress.


“Trevor! Don’t! Not right now! This whole thing, I’ve got to execute it just right! Goddess’s Domestic timetable! You’ll get me all flustered and our guests are out there! Get some wine to them, will you?” But she gave him a playful slap as he turned, letting him know the groping was not altogether unwelcome.


“Tie my apron, before you go back, I don’t want to get any grease on my dress as I slice the duck.”


The way she brandished a knife always made Trevor feel a strange mixture of elated confusion. Her precision with cooked meats, preceded always by the sound of the knife blade on the steel, like a shrill whisper, made the backs of his knees feel weak.


“My castle! My very own home, forever!” Sarah thought, every time she walked in the door during the Domestic Goddess year, when the dream was in the making.




———



				Then finally, she landed her dream job—a staff editorial position at 
Travel + Leisure
—that had her on assignment for weeks, sometimes a month at a time, having to brace herself every time she came home for the disorder Trevor made of things when she wasn’t there.


It took great fortitude for Sarah to swallow the shocking disarray upon returning home. Knowing she’d have to spend two days cleaning, putting her horse blinders on so she could focus on what was truly important—being the loving wife Trevor needed her to be, making up for being away so much more than was good for him.


If she let the disappointment reign, her time at home would start with a fight that had no resolution. So, Sarah would find herself apologizing for being disgusted and frustrated at what he’d made of the house, for holding expectations that were unfair. For setting Trevor up for failure.


“I can’t manage on my own, Sarah, you know that. I need 
you to be here to take care of things. If you choose to be away, you can’t expect me to keep up with all of it while you’re gone. I’m not good at this stuff like you are. Why do you have to heap all these expectations on me and then treat me like shit when you get home because you feel shitty about it?”


For a brief stint of time—three years—she worked her dream job, managing to live two dreams simultaneously. In one of the dreams, she and Trevor were trying for a baby.


They tried and tried and tried—four miscarriages—until the specialist made it clear that trying would not be enough if Sarah kept up the hectic lifestyle. She would need to pare it down if she wanted to be successful.


Sarah was given new instructions. And truth be told, after three years, she had had enough, hadn’t she? Now that the dream job was edging in on her dream life, maybe, she was coming to realize, the dream job was too much. Her body overstressed, her doctor, and her second-opinion doctor, told her. Nothing exactly wrong that wouldn’t let the babies take.


So, she was going to be home again for a while. Taking a break. A sabbatical of sorts—a perfect time for redesign! Intentionality, Sarah knew, was key in creating twoness as one. She had done that with the swan décor, but they were no longer newlyweds. It was time for Sarah to graduate from Domestic Goddess to Divine Mama.


Perhaps, in fact, the immovability of the décor had been part of the awful holding pattern? Sarah needed to move the energy! Transform the living space to make room for the next phase of the dream! She’d been too focused on her 
own work to see the full picture. And truth be told, Trevor hated the swan décor. Not even a month following the reveal, he had begun to complain about the clutter. About how it stalled his creativity to see Sarah’s busy style everywhere he looked. Could she not keep it more contained?


She moved all the over-the-top Sarah stuff to her office corner, mounting the swan bracket shelves to hold her notebooks. They had too many books now, anyway, for the small bookshelves. Trevor wanted a library in the living room to help clear some space in his study, and Sarah agreed. It would look classy. They decided on built-in white shelving. Trevor would organize the books. Sarah got to pick the contractor and arrange all the materials. Sarah splurged and also had the contractor build bookshelves up the stairwell, where she could store the books Trevor didn’t want on display.


Sarah’s office corner was getting cramped, but she knew how to work wonders in small spaces, given the cabin she’d grown up in. Her corner harkened back to childhood—a small magical nook in which to create ordered abundance. A small Sarah ecosystem. Trevor needed emptiness to move around in, whereas Sarah needed to feel like she was in a nest of gathered materials to create.


When first setting up the house, Sarah felt a shared office would be the fairest arrangement, but it had ultimately been decided that Trevor would get the office since he needed total and utter concentration to write.


Trevor let the house go to shit when Sarah wasn’t there, but he liked Sarah to keep it tidy. They needed to have a 
house they could invite people into that would not completely overwhelm the senses. His office, on the other hand, was a place he could let his own superstitious chaos reign. Before each new project he cleared the slate, but when a new story began, the creative madness built up around him in a kind of mythic order. His piles were stacked in a very particular way that held great significance, and if he talked about that, or anyone moved anything, the entire structure of his work might collapse. He knew if Sarah was in the office with him that she would disturb things. There was no question of sharing an office.


They had talked about Sarah making her office in the third bedroom, but that wouldn’t work either because Sarah was adamant that she didn’t want the TV in the living room. It was important they have some private leisure space from one another! She had read that in one of her books about taking the right home design steps to making a marriage work. So in the end, putting Sarah’s office in the corner of the master bedroom just made the most sense. And later down the road, when Trevor got a promotion, or when Sarah was earning more, she could renovate the garage into a studio. As for the TV room, Sarah had already begun drawing up plans to split it, imagining a little nursery in her mind’s eye. She could feel the calmness of this space, where neither she, nor Trevor, but a whole new living breathing entity—her future baby—would dream beautiful baby dreams.




———


Ariadne had picked apart the décor the one time she came to visit, just after Sarah’s Divine Mama renos. A painful sting.


Sarah had been so eager to show off her home to Ariadne, so excited when the stars aligned for Ariadne to make a trip to visit during the same week that Trevor was away at a conference.


“Wow! Grown-up, Sarah! So grown-up. I don’t recognize you anywhere. I mean, it’s just so put-together. I can see you feel pleased by it. But, um, I don’t know, maybe it just feels—consolidated? Maybe that’s not the right word? I’m looking for Sarah and I see you here in front of me, but I don’t see you in the house somehow.”


“Juxtaposition—not consolidation. Isn’t that obvious? It seems obvious to me,” Sarah protested.


“Oh, sweetie, forgive me! Sorry. Manners. God, I’m so excited to see you! I’ve never seen your grown-up home is all. Silly of me, I know. I guess I was expecting to walk in and find you in your room back on Chester, all curled up in your little orange velvet armchair with your headphones on and a textbook in your lap, straining your eyes in the dim gleam from your Christmas lights, steaming mug of tea beside you!”


“I’ve still got the trunk!” Sarah exclaimed, and she led Ariadne to her and Trevor’s bedroom, where Ariadne could see the small space the Sarah she knew still inhabited, all cramped up in the corner.


Shortly following this visit, Ariadne mailed Sarah a photograph that Sarah had not known existed—a polaroid 
of the glowing mobile of thrifted wedding dresses spinning in the centre of their old living room, hanging from a papier-mâché umbrella above the open trunk—one of the pieces they’d put together for their “Beyond the Bride Party,” the night of Sarah and Trevor’s first kiss.


Sarah’s trunk, that had been her sleeping place as an infant, her dress-up box as a child, that she’d brought with her to the city, full of the things she’d thought she might need for her student life, that she and Ariadne had transformed into a costume bin of defunct wedding gowns. Now her memento box. It held her letters from Ariadne—so many now after years of correspondence; her own wedding dress—carefully wrapped in crêpe and zipped into a plastic sarcophagus, so time could not undo the magic woven into place in the beaded pearl bodice; a set of badger-hair paintbrushes her father had made for her; sachets of dried lavender that almost totally erased the musty smell the trunk once had from the time before it was hers.


Looking at the photograph of the wedding dresses made Sarah feel—very briefly, for a moment, just for a flash—like some part of her, or perhaps many parts, were not after all present in her domestic arrangement.


It disturbed her, this feeling. This energy inside her that made her want to go into the living room and start knocking things off the shelves, spatter paint all over the walls, break a few dishes and use the shards to etch designs into the drywall. “How absurd!” she quickly thought. Ariadne! That troublemaker. Trevor always called her that. A troublemaker.


“A woman who stirs up trouble,” he’d say, when Sarah mentioned her. Speaking with a voice that hurt Sarah somewhere inside that she refused to touch too closely—an old raw feeling from when Trevor had loved Ariadne and Ariadne had loved Sarah and Sarah had loved them both, but had chosen Trevor. This future. Trevor assuring Sarah that he would love her for the duration, as a husband loves a wife. A love they would grow over time, unlike the infatuation Ariadne had been. “She can never give you the life you want, Sarah,” he’d said once. “You know that.”


“Yes, trouble,” Sarah thought. “Reminding me of another life.”


Still, the photograph jolted her. She had loved that life. How truly happy she had been to live there in that house with Ariadne, every day a delight. Ariadne had been so fun to play house with.


She did not seem convinced when Sarah explained that her tastes had matured and resources to buy things had altered since the days when twelve-dollar wedding dresses and two-dollar garage sale ceramic wall hangings pushed the limits of extravagance of what could be had, but Sarah knew this was because Ariadne found the whole concept of marriage distasteful.


Ariadne had long ago vowed she would never marry. She had invited Sarah to vow with her, and though some part of Sarah had wished to be as courageous as Ariadne, as sure of her own independence, she found that she could not make 
this promise. Because somewhere inside Sarah there lived a dream. The dream she was living here, in this house, with Trevor. These other vows that she had made. Sacrosanct vows written in law, whereas Ariadne’s vows were lawless.


Perhaps Ariadne is jealous that your dream has come true
, a voice offered from inside.


Sarah put the photograph away in the trunk.




———


Sarah mulled almost obsessively over Ariadne’s comments, mentally writing a series of responses that never made it to the page—wanting to explain herself, to have Ariadne understand and accept that Sarah was truly happy. Wanting her to see that marriage required sacrifices, sometimes selflessness, revising the dreams one might have held for oneself along the way if they didn’t fit with the other person’s dreams. Each person supporting the dreams of the other.


Weeks later, halfway through drafting yet another response, she realized that Ariadne was not her audience. Though she loved her friend dearly, their paths had diverged.


But this didn’t mean there wasn’t an audience out there! An audience of women like Sarah, who would get what she was talking about. Who were trying, as she was, to make the best lives possible for their families, no matter what life threw at them.


She did not have to defend herself to Ariadne, who would see through her defences and make her own interpretations anyway. Interpretations Sarah did not have the heart for.


Sarah was home. She needed to focus on what was truly important now that she’d set the stage for the next phase. Her life with Trevor, her desire to become a mother. Her marriage was suffering because she’d gotten sidetracked by her own ambitions and now her body was chronically stressed. Sarah was sensitive, Trevor often reminded her following the horrendous incident that had left them both so shaken. She needed to take better care of herself.


She knew she could work harder than anyone! But she wanted to have a baby. And each time she lost one, a little more doubt crept in that maybe she couldn’t, after all, work harder than anyone and still keep it all together.


The last one—on the plane on the way back from Singapore—had made her see how truly fragile and untenable things had become.


She had come home early to surprise Trevor. Blood all over the goddamn seat. Fifteen weeks. Her longest yet. She had been so sure. So sure this one was going to stick, had even started talking to her. She was singing when the cramps came. Just softly, softly, too quiet to even be a whisper. And it had been a fucking nightmare. Everyone on the plane witnessing her body’s failure to hold on to her baby. The plane had made an emergency landing in Montreal.


A full twenty-four hours had passed before the hospital had been able to contact Trevor. He had apparently lost his phone charger. And he hadn’t been expecting her for another week. She’d spent two nights alone in the hospital, hemorrhaging enough that she needed a blood transfusion. 
But at some point, she’d awoken from the terrible nightmare to find that Trevor was there. Holding her hand. And they had cried together for a long time.


Each miscarriage had escalated Sarah’s desperation. Her body not only protesting, but upping the stakes every time, dragging her along a little longer, building hope, and then digging her deeper into anguish, until her hormones surged once again, stringing her hope along, tangling up her thinking, making her want beyond all reason to try again, until this final one, making a humiliating show of it, for all to see. Grounding the fucking plane!


Sarah could not keep going on this trajectory. Trevor had been beside himself. How could he focus on his work when he was being called at the office, having to take a week off to drive to Montreal to pick Sarah up and bring her home because she could not get on a plane? He was totally on board when the doctor suggested Sarah take time off to recuperate after what he referred to as the “Singapore Incident.”


What began as a leave of absence turned into a career overhaul when Sarah discovered blogging—divinely inspired by another former journalist who wrote on the life transformation she had manifested by taking charge of the doom and gloom with a positive mindset framework, coaching herself to a successful home-based career as a blogger.


Really? This kind of thing was possible?


Only if you believed in it!


Sarah knew how to believe in things!




———


Blogging helped shift the narrative. Ridding Sarah of the darker thoughts that crept in when she allowed herself to travel into the future—all her hopes and dreams hijacked by swirling terrors blowing in from the past that Sarah needed to sweep out.


She decided on a slightly ironic persona that would keep it light, but with a kind of snarky humour that embraced positivity while acknowledging its limitations. 
Sarah at Home.


She could see it all perfectly! An innovative platform for undertaking an aesthetic exploration of artistry in women’s domestic expression, while simultaneously challenging negative perceptions of female domesticity. She wanted it to be serious, but also funny and practical, and popular enough that she could make a bit of money on the side for her Divine Mama projects, so Trevor could not complain about her spending his money on home design expenditures.


She would will belief into being!




———


Between steps outlining the exact approach needed to perfectly execute her grandmother’s delectable buttermilk biscuit recipe, Sarah mused on what it meant to be a home’s keeper, comparing marble, wooden, and glass rolling pins in tapered, cylindrical, or handled styles for her grandmother’s recipe, monetizing her content with affiliate posts, and describing how she felt contemporary care work could be both beautiful and radical.


Sarah’s extensive contacts from her former life as a journalist provided a significant platform from which to launch.


Within a year, Sarah was earning far beyond what she’d initially envisioned.


Within two, with a handful of significant sponsors, her platform had expanded exponentially.


Sarah was gaining recognition, earning enough that she was already blogging about her upcoming garage redesign—a home office that would even allow her to hire an assistant to help manage demands for increased content.


SARAH IN THE GARAGE!


You make it work.


You know how it is. You take what you’ve got and you make it work the way you want it to. It isn’t exactly what you want, but you know down the line it will be. That’s what you keep in mind. Do I want to be in this corner of my bedroom bringing together all these beautiful things for you, dear readers? These inspirations for your homes that you are working with to make it work the way you want it to? Not exactly. It wasn’t what I wanted, but I compromised, knowing down the road it would pay off. Hubby got the office, because if he’d been in the corner, I’d never have heard the end of it! He’d never have fit anyway, and then I would have had to sleep with his books on my face! Sex between the sheets and his manuscripts in progress? 
No Thank You!

And you know what, I love this corner! The corkboard, my father’s trunk. The salmon houndstooth motif painted on the wall (see October 15th’s post for how-to). January 18th’s desiccated flower accents collected from neighbourhood winter 
gardens—the effect would not have been as remarkable in a bigger space! It would have seemed insignificant. Small is a simple pathway for imaginative tweaks!


But, my dear, sweet, design-savvy sweethearts. IT IS 
Time! Garage Redesign!

I’m planning an IRL gala once it’s all said and done for those of you nearby enough to dress up and pop a cork with me to celebrate! (Open to theme suggestions!) There will be sourcing spreadsheets and budgeting tips and designers-to-go-to for legacy pieces some of us will only ever dream of. But hey, dreaming is half the fun, or sometimes all of it!

And then, Amelie stuck.


A Creature


With Amelie on the way, Sarah’s focus shifted. She could not let the fear engulf her. She had to begin preparing so she could become the best mother in the world now that her miracle was on the way! She had brought this miracle into being! She was ready for anything!


There was no longer time for design blog growth; Sarah now had greater priorities.


Mom blogs assured her that with proper planning so much unnecessary struggle could be avoided, so that if unexpected things happened, which they certainly would, a new mother could still be prepared, thus keeping everything on track—expectant moms like an army of preppers 
for the nigh times of good housekeeping after the new-baby apocalypse.


Sarah filled the chest freezer with soups, casseroles, muffins, and energy balls to basically last until Amelie went to college. She’d toyed with the idea of a second freezer, but Trevor had balked. “Freezer burn, Sarah.”


Three months of pre-baby prep work Sarah was grateful she had done, because the first month of Amelie she could basically do nothing, given the infection that set up camp in what had once been a very distinct space between her anus and vulva that had become, after the birth, a fissure of infectious bacterial activity reaching almost entirely between the two, following what Sarah overheard the obstetrician describe to the resident doctor in a hushed whisper as “ungodly tearing that sometimes occurs in cases when forceps are used to assist delivery.”


Sarah had to stay prone, manoeuvring painfully to lie on her side and nurse. Which left more up to Trevor than he had ever imagined having to manage. Because whereas Sarah had been prepping for what seemed like her entire life for this, Trevor had not imagined the baby would be real until the moment it came out.




———


During Amelie’s first weeks of life, Trevor dreamed over and over again a horrible dream. A secret nightmare he could never reveal to Sarah, instead scrawling it into one of his notebooks—material to use in a future story for a character he’d not yet written.




I stood at the edge of the lake watching the white swan, knees weak with beauty.


Each day I returned, weakened a little more—


time’s shadow pulling me to earth.


By the fifth day I was prone.


Still, I returned—


crawling, dragging my decrepit body.


Beauty called me.


Making me delirious—


I could not resist.


By the eighth day the swan started singing, or maybe it had been singing all along, and I had not heard because my ears had not yet been weakened enough to hear its range—


By the tenth day I could no longer move anything but my head, now seeing clearly.


The swan had grown human breasts and was suckling a wild thing. A baby brought up from the depths that would feed there for eternity, milk draining the white swan of its purity.


It turned to look in my direction, seeing through me as though I did not exist.


The swan was looking down at the wild thing as though it was an angel.


I saw the demon behind the baby’s eye, felt its hunger draining the white swan’s beauty.


Knowing it would feed until the swan lost white, seeking 
the blood that had fed it before the swan brought it up from the depths.


Weak as I was, my aim was off.


I aimed for the wild thing, killing the white swan instead.




For the first weeks of Amelie’s life, Trevor lived in a confusing oscillation of love and terror, doing the best he could to be there for Sarah. He adored and feared little Amelie. Sometimes settling into the deepest surety he had ever felt when he lay back with her on his chest and she found comfort from him, her small baby breaths grounding deep into his heart, felling them both to slumber.


He had never loved anything more than he loved this baby. And yet, he also found her horrendous, feeling utterly powerless to give her the life she demanded from Sarah, pulsing need and hunger as though a cosmic force from the tiniest amalgamation of bone and flesh he had ever seen. Amelie demanded of Sarah, and Sarah demanded of Trevor. She needed his help, he knew this. And he was willing to give it. His child! His child alive in the world.


Yet weeks wore into months wore into years and the distraction of needs did not lessen, only seemed to grow along with the baby. Distractions he had to combat if he was ever going to get anything done. A constant battle in this new reality Sarah had brought to life, threatening to overtake him. He needed to be vigilant if he was to maintain course.




OceanofPDF.com

















Chapter Four


Life Gets Real




Organic Crossovers



				Two short years after the first baby, a second—another little girl! Time had passed in days and nights of fluids and ruptured sleep, celebratory milestones, and thousands of photographs of Amelie doing every new thing an infant does for the first time in the world on its way to toddlerhood, whittled down to the most telltale shots on Sarah’s socials: Amelie walking for the first time, Amelie with first-birthday cake all over her face, Amelie blowing on a dandelion head, delighted by the flying seeds, Amelie going down the big slide at the park, face alight with fear become elation, Amelie, frowning a little, trying to smile for Mama, holding a little sign labelled “Big Sister” to announce the big news—flushing Sarah with dopamine-hits of approval from 
supportive readers anticipating the expanded content to come now that there would be two. Meanwhile, tenure and promotion for Trevor, and a next book well on the way!


Sure, they lived far away from any supports—Sarah’s mom on the other side of the country and her sister on another continent, Trevor’s family a few hours’ drive north and not really ones to intrude on the couple’s privacy, and the few colleagues Sarah had counted as friends childless, not really available, with hectic lives on the go—but Sarah had a Mom and Baby group she went to once a week, and her online community was there whenever she needed it!


There had been some struggles, but they had persevered to become the family Sarah had always dreamed of. The real family she now had, in a nice house full of nice things in the city. The family she had pretended was her family as a child holed up in a hollow in a birch tree while her parents seethed inside an unfinished house—Sarah’s mom drawing demons out of her father, sometimes lashing them in return, while Sarah escaped into a dreamworld outside.


How far Sarah had come! How tempered, her and Trevor’s words to one another, so aligned—almost entirely free of disagreement.


The kids! The house—designed with highest-quality elements to fit the functional and aesthetic needs of the style-forward nuclear kin group. Tasteful ensembles, a good hairstylist (there had been a few mishaps along the way, but now they had Chloë, who they tipped well, knowing how truly valuable she was), children in eco-chic ensembles that 
Sarah special-ordered from websites promoted on 
Swan Mom
—the blog that had evolved organically from 
Sarah at Home
, following Sarah’s life-upgrade from Domestic Goddess to Divine Mama.


Home design was background stuff; necessary, but only as set for the plot of family life unfolding within. Her content now focused on her two bright children dutifully concentrating on Sarah’s clever learning activities, engaging in imaginative free-play, or hilariously hijacking over-planned educational flops, because they were children, after all. It couldn’t all be perfect, but it was an impeccably curated family!


Sarah’s socials attested to it. With regularly updated links to 
Swan Mom
, where one could always go to learn how to pull it all together, as Sarah did, with Trevor’s unwavering support.


Their library featured heavily. Trevor’s extensive and eclectic collection took up the whole open-concept living room/dining room wall. Trevor loved picking passages for Sarah to use on 
Swan Mom
. Sarah loved endorsing titles and working in quotes alongside photographs of meals recreated from inspiration found in the pages of her favourite books, where there was always ample material to riff off of. She thrilled at pairing passages from books with staged replays of the kids enacting the parts of a variety of literary characters.


The internet allowed for so many organic crossovers in life! Instead of casually name-dropping books in conversation, Sarah could take a simple photo and post it, drawing 
attention to her collection of parenting books written by every expert who’d ever thought the word 
child
, accompanied by succinct yet encouraging cutlines with convenient links to specialist sites, where parents could access coaching and consultation services. Sarah’s learning wasn’t just for her! It helped other moms find the support they needed when they felt they had nowhere to turn. Helpful, informed, and pleasantly housed in her curio feature on the staircase—the little cubbies she so loved in the wall that climbed alongside the stairs.


How many precious photographs of the girls feeling it out with Mama had been taken on that staircase? It was exceedingly annoying to have to vacuum in between the stairs and book crevices, having to pull out all the books to make sure there were no visible dust bunnies in the shots, but it kept Sarah on her housecleaning toes.


The blog had been going six years. Six years! It was a total success, earnings steadily on the rise. There had been a slight drop following Arlynn’s birth, when things were tough and Sarah could not keep up. But she had recovered, as she always did. 
Swan Mom
 had prevailed. She now had the most adorable six-year-old, as well as a spunky three-and-a-half-year-old. Her proudest delights around which to build an amazing world.


Swan Mom
 On
 Swann’s Way


And soon, mechanically, oppressed by the gloomy day and the prospect of a sad future, I carried to my lips a spoonful 
of the tea in which I had let soften a piece of madeleine. But at the very instant when the mouthful of tea mixed with cake-crumbs touched my palate, I quivered, attentive to the extraordinary thing that was happening in me. A delicious pleasure had invaded me, isolated me, without my having any notion as to its cause. It had immediately made the vicissitudes of life unimportant to me, its disasters innocuous, its brevity illusory, acting in the same way that love acts, by filling me with a precious essence; or rather this essence was not in me, it was me. I had ceased to feel I was mediocre, contingent, mortal. Where could it have come to me from—this powerful joy?


—Marcel Proust (translated by Lydia Davis,
 The Way by Swann’s
)


How could I not? Of course, I had to! A perfect tidbit dear Proust left us to follow on
 Swan Mom. 
Les “petites madeleines” mais oui! Amelie and Arlynn delighted in preparing these cakes with me (Proust’s maman’s recipe follows).


I used the petites madeleines tin moulds picked up in an antique shop in Paris on the honeymoon—serendipity for this post waiting in a future I had not yet imagined? Trevor was not pleased to have to fit the trays into the backpacks! From my archives, you can see him pouting, holding the tins after I’ve gleefully purchased them, his other hand holding his copy of
 Swann’s Way 
picked up at the bookshop next door, pretending he’s being doubled over by the added weight of the tins and not all the books he’s carrying!


Since we’re time-travelling, here’s me, convincing him later in the day it will be worth it, biting into an inspirational petite madeleine at a nearby café. Ever the trooper, Trevor carried these tins across Europe for us. Proust would have been proud.


The girls are dressed in adorable period dresses from one of my fashion mom faves, 
Pinafore Pretties
. This mama makes old-timey looks in modern eco-materials kids can play in that you feel good about. Laurelei collects vintage patterns and knows her fabrics—all her playsuits, dresses, and pinafores (time to bring those back!) are machine washable.


My heart lifts with Proust’s “powerful joy” to have captured this perfect moment of the girls munching their madeleines and sipping Proust’s tea, no doubt totally unaware of mediocrity or contingent mortality. I hope, dear readers, you too are inspired to pay special heed to the pleasures of motherhood that manifest in moments like these—all the pieces aligning extraordinarily, as they did today!


To the madeleines! But first, let me add that the moment would have been nowhere near as impeccable without the 
Deerheart Ceramics
 tea sets made by Milanda in Montana! Who collects clay from the Clark Fork River to craft unique pieces embossed with local fauna and printed with transfers of the coolest little critters. These fairy-tale-meets-art-school treasures are almost indestructible, so you can rest assured they won’t meet innocuous disaster as the littles sip! Pieces you’ll hold on to for life!




———


Sarah worked hard for “powerful joy.” Weeks she had put into staging this. Holding back from revealing the pinafores until the last minute, once it was all set up, so the girls would be excited to put them on. But Amelie had refused. Because Arlynn’s pinafore was nicer than hers and she wanted Arlynn’s!


Sarah had taken so long to decide. Had waffled between the fox and the bear, thinking probably it would be best to get them both the same, but then at the last minute had chosen different ones, wanting to promote the different designs, perhaps land a few more linked sales with the added inspiration of a fox-and-bear conversation.


“But it doesn’t fit, honey. You can’t wear this one. Don’t you see, Mama got you the one with foxes on it. Like Monsieur Renard!”


“Monsieur Renard doesn’t look like that. This fox is bad. It’s a bad fox. I want the one with the kind bear Arlynn has.”


“Mama can get you a kind bear one later. We can send this one back, but for today, can you put this one on for the party Mama set up? You can help make the little shell cakes, les petites madeleines! And eat as many as you want after we do the photos.”


“You always give better ones to Arlynn!” Amelie shouted, grabbing the fox pinafore out of Sarah’s hands and throwing it at Arlynn, trying to pull the bear one out of Arlynn’s hands as Arlynn held tight, and then Arlynn fell, smacking her head on the floor, screeching, and Amelie stumbled back, knocking her head on the table where the tea sets 
sat—two little fox cups and two little bear cups tumbling off the table—one of each broken apart—sharp white shards on the shiny blue tiles Sarah had lovingly grouted with white contrasting lines. Both of the girls now piercing her ears with horrendous destructive wails.


Sarah stood, dumbfounded, rage rushing fast, planting her feet, screeching back, “Amelie, look what you’ve done! You’ve ruined it! You’ve ruined it all!” Making them both screech louder. And then, screaming louder than them both, because she was bigger and could drown them out. As big and loud as her father! Flattening everyone else in the room!


And it worked. They both stopped, looking up at her in shock.


Draining her completely—her daughters there, on the verge of shattering. Not pretty at all.


“Babies, babies, I’m so sorry. Oh sweeties. Mama’s right here.” Pulling them in. Holding them close. Getting small with them on the ground, breathing them back to safety, out of the horrible brokenness. She would make it work. She would make it better. It was recuperable. Just a little mishap. And after the tears, after it had all been cried out, they managed, the three of them, to make a game of the slightly broken tea party, and Sarah got her shots.


Swan Mom’s Dad Content



				Trevor had been reluctant to commit to the dad content Sarah had insisted on at 
Swan Mom
’s inception, but Sarah had convinced him, reasoning that it would differentiate 
what she had to offer from what other moms were offering and up the potential to further monetize her posts. Framing it in economic terms had been a surefire way to get Trevor to contribute.


“I have so much on my plate already, Sarah, now you want me to contribute to your blog on top of it all?”


“It will be fun, Trev. And maybe you can stop taking yourself so seriously for a few minutes and write something enjoyable! It would really set my project apart. We need to hear more fathers’ voices. You know that as well as I do. When I hear you talk about how you want to do better for Amelie, be more present. How you learn about the more tender parts of yourself by giving your love to Amelie and helping me take care of her. Other fathers need to hear that too.”


He had been so amenable those first few months on parental leave after Amelie’s birth, feeling things he had never felt before, being present in the home—for Sarah—in entirely new ways. Feeling this burgeoning caregiving role inside of him. It had been a revelation.


Though he had been so reluctant to take the leave. So reluctant.


He had finally landed the tenure-track job when Sarah had gotten pregnant. In that tenuous phase before tenure when he had to really give it his all. It wouldn’t look good. But it was university policy. He 
could
 do it. Other fathers could do it, though they rarely did. He was better than those other fathers. He could take the leave and come back strong. He owed this to Sarah. They had settled on five months. 
Five months was what he could manage if he wanted to stay on track. And even this was asking a lot. A lot he was giving to Sarah here. More than any of the other men in his department.


He could see how much work Sarah had done to bring their child 
to life
. But he felt wholly inadequate, so unsure about his place in it all.


This creature he loved would most certainly die without Sarah! Because how would he keep her alive? He had no idea. There was all this vulnerability that he 
wanted
 to express. It felt kind of deranged, but sincere too. Was this what other fathers felt like? What was this emergent desire for sincerity now that he had brought another being to life? Trevor found himself asking questions like this often. Questions that had never once entered his realm of thought before. About what other men might be feeling. He thought often enough about what other men thought of him, how they measured up to one another, but what they might be feeling themselves? The thought had never entered his mind until he first held Amelie.




———



				Whenever Sarah felt like real and virtual life were a little misaligned, she would hyperlink back to the very first post of Trevor’s to remind herself of the firm, solid roots of parenthood that had started it all, feeling deeply how when things were hard it was because Trevor was afraid. Just like the children were often afraid. And she could see and remember that and draw him back again toward the love 
she knew was there within him. Love they could share, from which she could draw the nourishment she and the girls also needed. Nourishment she and other moms sometimes needed to remind themselves they still existed in the miracle of life when the marriage and all sense of any previous reality of independent existence sometimes got a little lost.


Letter to a Daughter


by Trevor Hart


Gazing upon your newborn face for the first time, I wept. Hit with a potent sense of realness I’d never before felt.


I had known you were there, felt you move inside your mother’s womb, but I had not known you’d be real. It sounds strange, but that is what I felt.


A certain truth that my life would never again be mine alone. It was terrifying. Tears of fear as much as love—or perhaps this kind of elemental love cannot be separated from fear? I felt it in my body—you—made of the same matter as me. I now had something to lose that I feared might also kill me.


And then a question—how could I be responsible for this?


I could not fail.


I would fail. How could I not fail given my total inadequacy?


And yet, there you were. And I loved you.


How could my love be equal to you?


It brought to mind Rilke:


“It is also good to love: because love is difficult. For one human being to love another human being: that is perhaps the 
most difficult task that has been entrusted to us, the ultimate task, the final test and proof, the work for which all other work is merely preparation.”


I will work for you, Amelie. As unprepared as I am.


—Papa


This first post had put Trevor on a roll. A confessional mode he had never thought himself capable of. Which he would have scoffed at before the new feeling Amelie had opened in him. Subsequent posts prompted him to reflect on his role tending to Sarah, opening up this new mental space from which he could express with utmost sincerity how he loved the feeling of Amelie napping on his bare chest—the weight of her new body pressing down on his heart, like love flowing between two separate yet united selves. He could write this on Sarah’s blog without the sticky feelings of need and want and privation that always wheedled into his work—the push-pull dance he had felt his entire life, like revelation, then anguish, satisfaction, and finally disgust. The way people inevitably disappointed. Never living up to the potential they initially held. Amelie was all potential. But she was something more, too. She would never disappoint.


There was fear, yes, but no anguish in loving Amelie.




———


Except in the night.


In the night when she would not sleep, robbing him of his dreams, or turning into a demon baby.


But that wasn’t Amelie!


That was his own ugly psyche making a beautiful thing into a monster.


A thing he had to protect Amelie from.


Looking at her peacefully sleeping he often wondered when nightmares began. Did babies have them? Did they wake because they felt sinister things, or just because they were hungry? Hunger and the pain of being in frail human bodies the only sinister things they yet knew.


Over time the tone within Trevor shifted. Darker thoughts that emerged in the night, lingered on now in the days. There was so much he sometimes wished to say in his dad posts that he could not say. Darker thoughts sweeping innocence away, like the recurrent nightmare of killing the mama swan.




———


For the five months of his parental leave with Amelie, Trevor had been consistent enough with the dad content, truly believing he had turned a new leaf, that Amelie had changed him.


Sarah was all admiration, offering him lavish praise!


But once back at work, his commitment waned.


It was not a drive from within that had kept him contributing, he realized, but the payback he got when moms swooned—firing off comments about how good a man he was, how beautifully he articulated himself, how deeply their husbands had been moved by his words. But as hits do, the thrill of approval had waned over time, not steady enough to keep him charged. It became more and more of a chore. 
As irrelevant to him as most of the things Sarah blogged about. A farce he was now expected to uphold, underpinned by a disturbing reality there was no room for anywhere in Sarah’s sophisticated hyper-positive virtual world.


Amelie was very, very real. Requiring so much of Sarah. And Trevor was tired.


Real life pounced like a predatory cat on the tail end of parental leave. Whereas once the dad content had spurred good feeling between him and Sarah, it had grown quickly enough into yet one more thing for her to hassle him about. Why had he ever committed? Then, one day, a rare miracle! Sarah approaching with a request that freed, rather than asking more.


“Trev, I feel like you just aren’t interested in the dad content anymore, like I’m always niggling at you to get it done, stressing about whether or not you’re going to. And then I feel resentful about it when you keep putting it off. I hate that. This is supposed to be fun.”


“I know, babe. I just can’t seem to get to it. I’ve got so many other things—my writing, assignments, reviews, policy docs, grading. I’m not getting paid for this. It’s your thing, not mine. But I know it’s important to you, so I will. I’ll keep at it.”


“Well, I was thinking, maybe you shouldn’t? Maybe once in a while, if you feel like it. If you feel inspired. But I want to help you out, take things off your plate. This is maybe a little weird, but—would you be okay if I kept it going, writing the posts for you?”


Off the fucking hook! “Um, sure, babe, if you’d really be okay with that? It would remove a lot of pressure for both of us. Then you could get what you want and I don’t have to feel like a failure for not pulling through for you.”




———


By the time his second parental leave came around, the dad content had long since faded from Trevor’s awareness.


The thought of contributing again did not even arise.


The second time around, he was only able to spare three months. Three months that seemed like one long fog. He knew by now fatherhood was no true redemption. Trevor loved Arlynn! Of course he did. She was his baby too, but he was still going to be Trevor a few months down the road when real life started up again. He had understood this after Amelie.


And Arlynn did not delude them into blissful baby miracle fantasies as Amelie had. This baby was fucking difficult! Allergic to Sarah’s milk. Allergic to almost everything she ate—a year before she slept more than half an hour straight on any given night. Trevor busted his balls being there for Sarah in the ways he could. Trading off with her during the day and getting up for Amelie if she too woke in the night. But he also had a book to write that he was barely able to work on. He just couldn’t find the headspace with everything going on at home, keeping up with reading other people’s books so he stayed relevant, and students who needed his fullest attention at school. Who were gracious and rewarding, who were searching, as 
he still was, for threads of the miraculous. He adored and was adored by them; the rewards, when they came, were immeasurable, unexpected. The potential always there, but so rarely realized. A total contrast to the domestic entanglement of routine comforts and inconveniences that dragged him down into the domain of Sarah’s chaotic fantasy family world.


All magics waned over time, and what did he truly have to offer an infant? Sarah had it all under control. The children were her domain. He didn’t have the tolerance she did, and he had so much to accomplish. He played with Amelie to help, bounced Arlynn and held her for as long as she would accept before reaching for Sarah again. They had barely any interest in him anyway, always asking for Mama. He was grateful to head back to work when the three months were over.




———


After the first year of Arlynn’s life, Trevor took stock.


Was this all life was going to be from here forward?


Since this baby had been born, he’d basically been nonexistent, achieved nothing. No headway on the book; not really present with Sarah and the girls. In permanent distraction. He couldn’t be where he wanted—writing—and couldn’t give himself over to what family life required, either. So often he found himself retreating to his office, getting nothing done. Agitating. Wasting time doing “research” that led nowhere, procrastinating by reading books he could barely pay attention to, always chasing a comet’s tail.


His dreams were getting more and more off course. His first book had been a moderate success—a book of stories based on his and Sarah’s dreams, written during his M.F.A., revised and finally published shortly after he’d begun teaching. A creative escape he’d continuously plugged away at during the Ph.D. dissertation. He’d received enough attention to finally land the tenure-track job and had from there, with the publication of a few stories and poems in literary journals, alongside several academic articles, glided into tenure. He’d been on fire then! On fire! Completely enthralling. Truly alive. Unstoppable.


He’d envisioned something completely different for his second book. Yet all he could seem to manage were short pieces based on his nightmares.


Sarah was always trying to come up with solutions for him.


“You can reset the mind at any moment, Trevor. There are all sorts of things you can learn to do that. Resetting life, though? No matter what you do, you’re still going to be Trevor, finding yourself in the same mind under different circumstances.”


She could be so unhelpful sometimes. She had a fucking explanation for everything with all the goddamn reading she was always doing. When did he even have time to truly read anymore? But there was truth to it too, he knew. And he didn’t want to reset his life completely! He didn’t want to erase his family, just pause them. Pause them so he 
didn’t feel so overwhelmed. For them to be there again when he was ready, but not there all the time, always wanting things from him he didn’t have to give, leaving him no time for the things he was meant for.


Trevor knew he should try harder. Give what Sarah was saying a chance.


“I listened to the podcast you sent about mind chatter and the stories that limit what we think is possible on my ride to work. The one by—what was that guy’s name again?” Trevor said, and then went on without waiting for a reply, “That guy has almost the same voice in his head as me! And it floored me that I’m not the only one with that voice in my head! That inner critic. The asshole Trevor who’s always warning me of impending doom, agitating about how nothing is ever good enough, and how I can learn to tell him to fuck off. Draw on that other voice, the one that knows how much greatness there is in me. Listen to that voice instead. Follow that lead. The one that knows the real truth of who I am.”


Sarah replied, “I think it was more like giving him a big hug inside himself, wasn’t it? That was my takeaway. Not that you kick the inner asshole to the curb or focus on what you need to achieve to be great, but that you learn to give him a little love? You know, think of that guy as at one time being just like Arlynn and Amelie—tender and beautiful and worthy of love—just one part of you among many, a part that needs acceptance, maybe even so much more than 
the other parts that feel like the great parts of you? A part that has been deeply wounded and learned to talk to itself in ways that magnify rather than heal the pain?”


“Yeah, I guess,” Trevor replied, giving her a withering look. But instead of stopping, Sarah went on about other things the podcast expert had said and other important points about ways Trevor could change to like himself more and therefore become a better person. And Trevor tuned out, losing the content of what she was saying, disassociating because instead he was thinking about how she was always wanting him to be different than he was, and how he was never good enough for her standards, how she always had some ulterior motive that wasn’t about helping him achieve what he needed to achieve but about trying to manipulate him to be how she wanted him to be, and this was just another example of that, even though he had connected with what the guy said. Had felt seen by the man, or at least mirrored—a successful man, by all counts, big research lab at UPenn, a voice that made you take him as an authority so you believed everything he said was truth based on some natural quality of leadership and a high-profile academic job. This man had this voice inside him too, the inner asshole full of self-doubt about thwarted aspirations and truer visions who was always looking for someone to blame.


Trevor tried to look his inner asshole in the face and see little Amelie—bright and funny and full of life, full of need, and so scared sometimes, then brimming with joy, 
glee that bubbled, silliness and wiggles, and then so much anger, this little vacillating creature that most of all needed her mother’s love and adoration. Her mother’s, and her father’s, too. How she’d come to him, trying to draw him out, when he had so much on his mind. Sometimes he could see her through the fog, other times he’d push her away. Because he didn’t have time. And he knew Sarah was there. That she’d give Amelie what she needed. And he’d feel bad afterward, but he’d just push the badness away. Later. He’d connect with her later, when he had more time on his hands, when he’d done what he needed to do for the day, though he rarely felt like he had.


Trevor tried to find the boy he’d been inside himself, but it was like he was locked away or lost to time and Trevor could not feel him. All he could remember was feeling like a weak fuck of a thing always in need of something he could not seem to grasp, not getting what it was he needed time and time again, having to find his own ways of getting what he wanted.


It was a stupid exercise. Frustrating. He wasn’t a goddamn child anyway. Couldn’t even remember what that had felt like. And when he had been, his mom hadn’t been like Sarah. Or other mom-blog-reading moms. All those Sarahs out there with their smug mom expressions knowing how to do everything right and their husbands were just supposed to fall in line with whatever new parenting thing was the 
it
 thing as though the children, now that they were born, were suddenly the only important thing in the world, as 
though it was totally forgotten that Sarah and Trevor’s lives had existed, and been full, and interesting, and a hell of a lot more enjoyable before the children came along, becoming the utter and total focus of everything for Sarah.


For Christ’s sake, the woman no longer even remembered how to fuck!


But she had known. She had known once.

Chapter Six


Summer Interlude




Sarah and Trevor came to an agreement.


They would split summer break.


Sarah would take the kids to Vancouver for the month of July to visit her mother so Trevor could have time to write. Come August, Trevor would take the kids for the long weekend to his parents’ place on his own so Sarah could recalibrate, after which she would come and join them all for a week at the Hart cottage along with Trevor’s sister Mimi and Trevor’s ten-year-old nephew—a kid Trevor had only seen once, as a baby, who he always thought of as Scott, because in the photos Eileen kept of him on the fridge, he always looked to Trevor like a Scottie dog. “His name is Randolph,” Sarah would say, each time Trevor, prompted by a new fridge photo, would ask Eileen, “How 
is
 Scott doing?” Mimi had moved to San Diego after law school and had been too busy working her way to senior partner to pay much attention to Trevor’s life, so why would he have any interest in hers?

The last few weeks of August, the girls would have day camp and Sarah and Trevor would alternate weekend parental duties—Trevor on Saturdays, Sarah on Sundays—time at home without kids during the week to focus on work and maybe a little kid-free fun on the side.


July went off without a hitch.

Morning over. Epic starts to a month of perfect days.


Everything triple-backed-up. Trevor was taking no chances with the magic flowing through him with this new book! Going so far as to save it front and centre on his desktop, prime real estate, letting it know it was the real writing that truly counted.

Chapter Seven


Fantasy Becomes Real




First Day of School


It was the first day of the fall semester. Students abuzz with the twin promises of intellectual enlightenment and classmates who might become more, uncertain how they would measure up in the competitive minefields of university success—marked by who one managed to socially bond with, who one might bring to bed, and how the course might affect one’s GPA—once a bowel-wrenching anxiety that had, with confidence gained from experience, become a thrill to Trevor. He well remembered the anticipation. Though now he was in the position of pulling the expectant strings, with so much more power to delineate the outcome.


Trevor thought of his beautifully crafted syllabus. He had done so much to plan for this semester! He’d had to 
cut out some of his favourite writers to make room for new content he’d never taught before. He worried that excluding some of the classics would leave gaps in the students’ education, but he knew it was important to shift the status quo. He thought of the steps he’d taken to consult with junior female colleagues—those who knew he had a feature on his wife’s mom blog. To whom he often lent an ear for conversations about how much they were expected to shoulder, feeling like they were competing in structures that were never set up for their benefit. Trevor thought of the trust they placed in him and how much effort he had put in on their behalf in departmental meetings the last few years, siding with them against some of their more defensive male colleagues about the importance of teaching female writers.
