To dare herself out of a confined life defined by everyone around her—a world she did not fit into and did not want to fit into—propelling herself into a wider life in which she could disperse, leak out, explode, even, if she desired. She could begin with her body as character, as stage, as stand-in for every woman punished for pursuing her desire in the myth of Ariadne, but then, instead of being redeemed by a male god, she would enact her own redemption.




———


For a few years now, Masha had been working as a waitress in a cocktail bar and writing poetry.


Oliver was always on her to go back to school before it was too late.


“It isn’t like having babies, Papa, it doesn’t get too late to go to school.”


“Oh, but it does, Mashenka. When you’re young you think you have all the time in the world, but you don’t. School isn’t just about the learning, it’s about the people you meet there too. The people who become friends you might stay connected to for life.”


Masha had looked over at Nikki, hoping for backup, but this time SM was on Papa’s side.


“It’s how I met Rika, after all. You never know where your studies will take you. You’ve been talking about wanting change, Masha, why not just give it a shot for a few semesters?”


“Do I really have to go to school to be a poet, Papa?” Masha felt she didn’t. But she knew they were right. What did she have to lose? She could use a change of scenery, Papa was paying, and she was eager to learn more, receive guidance, and meet other poets. It was just that she wanted to do things she wanted to do, not the things Papa wanted her to do! But she had to grow up sometime, and in this case it made no sense for her not to do it just because what she wanted happened to align with things Papa wanted.


The thought of sharing her poetry scared her in a way other performances didn’t. Because she wouldn’t be using her body as medium or distraction. Instead, she’d be sharing her voice, which she felt protective of, because it was vulnerable—tremulous sometimes, unlike her exultant body, which knew exactly how to feel.


Embodying The Husband



				PIT and Sarah were a little on the rocks. Probably because Sarah had not been kind 
every
 time to him for a while. She did not want to admit to herself how unkind she truly was. That every time she heard him speak—that old comforting friend—another voice inside her, a very angry voice, began telling PIT to shut the fuck up—
stop trying to drag me down—I don’t want to hear anything you have to say!


Sarah also did not wish to admit that what had started out as an occasional game of afternoon dress-up had become a compulsive habit that seemed to be taking possession of her now that she was back home and the year ahead, so full of promise in the spaciousness of summer—floating in a cool dark lake, holding hands with her beloved—now felt like a swan dive straight into a familiar fetid livestock pond.


Unkind Sarah now felt blurred in confounded certitudes as soon as she put Trevor’s pineapple button-up shirt on. Swaggering a bit when she slipped into his half-brogues, knotting the extra lengths of laces she brought with her for her sessions onto the ends of the laces already there, winding and tying them tight around her calves. Not PIT, not Sarah, not quite Trevor, but some liminal creature, slipping between the loops of the binding threads. The pressure from the ropes binding the transference in place.


Sarah gone.


Become Trevor at his desk thinking about the essays he still had to mark and the steaks Sarah had asked him to pick up from the butcher for the dinner party on Friday, and whether or not “E Unibus Pluram” was too dated for students, like maybe it would be better to just direct them to a YouTube video about the essay rather than assign them the essay, since it would give them a bit more depth than the Wikipedia page they would inevitably read instead of the essay, and they might actually watch the YouTube video, whereas it was likely that maybe only one-quarter of them would read the essay even halfway through, and was TV 
even a relevant idea to them? And was it even more not okay because of #MeToo? And where did Dr. Hart sit on the misogyny vs. genius divide? He couldn’t remove every misogynistic genius from his syllabi now that all his heroes were deemed to be monsters! Couldn’t they have an open and critical discussion about all this anyway? Should Dr. Hart have Dr. Dass come in and have a debate about it with the class?


Sometimes Sarah slipped in, trying to have a conversation with Trevor he did not want to have while he was getting ready to watch porn. Sarah’s mind drifting off-focus when Dr. Dass came to mind. Remembering the hearty debate at the department Christmas party she and Trevor had hosted last year. And how Ramona, one of Trevor’s grad students, had been there, also joining the discussion about whether a writer’s work should be measured against their life.


David Foster Wallace—no wonder her mind was drifting! Trevor had been defending him, arguing that being a critic and a reader were different, and that one could not omit the importance of literary innovation in Wallace’s work by holding it against the man’s life—that the two should not be conflated. Dr. Dass—Sammi—had been on the side of cancelling Wallace. Of removing his work from the classroom altogether—there were so many other authors one could include, whose work had been overlooked by the very same structures of oppression that privileged Wallace, celebrating him while overlooking the sometimes violent oppression he inflicted on the women he had sexual and 
intimate relations with. Meanwhile Ramona had offered another opinion, saying that the man’s work and life needed to be read in conjunction. To broaden the critical perspective. Suggesting that looking at both revealed things we might otherwise fail to fully register because as readers we are embedded in the structures, steeped in and sometimes unwittingly supporting them, without realizing it. That we read with the same invisible biases writers write with when we read from within dominance. Ourselves implicit in rape culture and the sexual oppression of women, refusing to acknowledge what is playing out right before us in a cultural framework that upholds toxic masculinity through the very notion that what a person writes, and how a person behaves, might be considered separately instead of as clear indications of what they truly believe.


“A mind making meaning symbolically is an act undertaken in a moral order,” Ramona had argued, “making belief palpable.”


Sarah had not fully understood then, and she did not fully understand now, the scope of what they’d been debating. Trevor had had her read Wallace’s 
Infinite Jest
 at some point in their twenties, but she had gotten lost. Not lost in the way she liked to be lost, but overwhelmed by a sense of flooded weariness that overtook her every time she tried to engage with the man’s mind.


It reminded her of listening to her father rave when she sat outside his cabin door. All the strange things that would come out of his mouth—the stories and words he’d never say 
in anyone else’s presence that spewed forth in endless fury, criticism and sometimes ongoing encyclopedic explanation in the presence of his wife and daughters that it seemed his demons demanded of him. All the blame falling on those around him. The structures that corrupted a man’s soul. Sometimes alternating shrieks and barks in French, a language Sarah could blessedly not understand, though the messages were clear enough without her needing to know the meaning of the words. There were words that sounded like they were in another language too. So few of these, which were less recognizable. These words seemed softer somehow, like they were trying to reach into his other words and draw him out from the deluge. How her father would vacillate between characters so seemingly at odds with one another and the world, and how Sarah would sit outside his door, outside his mind, hearing all of it, wanting to find some way to grab her father’s hand and lead him back into the world she saw. The world she knew he could see when he wasn’t stuck in this one. Because he was the one who had shown this other marvellous loving world to her, too.


“Focus, Sarah, focus
. 
You are in costume now.”


“PIT?”


They grabbed the mouse, but then, before the arrow landed on where they were trying to get to, their mind swung back to the other side of the Sarah-Trevor pendulum and she remembered that she had to think about what to pack for the girls’ school lunches and daycare snacks and what to buy or get Trevor to buy at the grocery store to go 
into those lunches and who would eat what and not eat what if you put it in their lunches and which were strictly no nuts and who had decided this week they were never eating carrots again after loving them last week and now no one would eat carrots but if she just slipped them in at school and other kids were eating carrots they might forget and eat the carrots anyway—


“No!”
 Trevor got so quickly bored of this line of thought, which was so inconsequential to him. Sarah would tell him if he got it wrong, which was annoying, but at least he didn’t have to put in the effort to remember, so it was probably a good trade-off.




———


“Rope it in!”


PIT slipped through, reaching down to tighten the laces.


All the Sarah thoughts squeezed out.

They already knew that “Shiloh’s Hangover” was Trevor’s retelling of a story he’d overheard one of his students recount to another about a fantastical hangover in which the pressure in his head was so loud it was like he was inside a washing machine. The protagonist had a washing machine for a head and was trying to relate to the world around him while also trying to figure out how to order a drink when all that would come out of his mouth were soap bubbles and his wife kept adding dirty laundry into the door that led to his brain every time she saw him.


And “The Face” was not one of Trevor’s best. It was a barely fictionalized account of Trevor’s infatuation with Ariadne in university. He hadn’t even bothered to change her name or his name in the story. Sarah he’d changed to Sara, for some reason. She had questioned him about this when he’d first shared it with her, and he’d gotten huffy, not answering directly.

“It’s somewhere in between. It’s called 
autofiction
. I’m going to add some more character details later. I got the auto part down now and I’m going to play with the themes and work the fiction in from here. What I want to know is how you feel about how I’m portraying you in the story.”

h
 in my name when you kept everyone else’s the same. It feels like I’m a caricature and the others are real people.”

Sarah had paused. Mystical wife? Why did that make her want to throw one of the kids’ toys at Trevor’s face? “Isn’t that song about Sara leaving him?”


“It’s about him loving her. Just not being able to live in real life together. About his enduring love for her.”


“The poor man,” Sarah had responded. “Living in real life is hard, but does Sara get to stop living in real life too, or does she have to stay in real life and take care of the kids while he goes on to write songs about her mystique? Does she get to experience being mystical, or does he just get to project his desires onto her that way?”


“You’re so critical, Sarah! This is Dylan, for god’s sake. The man is an icon. The song is a song for the ages! You used to put it on every single mix tape you made. I thought you loved it? I wrote it like that for you!”


“I used to, Trevor. I did. You’re not wrong about the way I used to be. But I hear it differently now.” Sarah had paused, then gone back to another question about the story—“I guess I’m just wondering what your angle is? What are you trying to say with it, or what are you trying to get at?”


“I don’t quite know yet. Something about absence, I think.”

And then my porn star became a person.


A student in my class with a name of her own! Masha Novak.

This girl has more real to her than I do at forty-three. her

Looking for the best in him all the time, trying to understand, to make the world better for him, but she had erred somewhere, somehow.


When he was looking at her, he was looking at his own reflection—Sarah obscured by her mirroring of him with all the neurons in her body, attuning to the emotions of this person in front of her, giving him her all, feeling it along with him.


When she looked at him, she never encountered her own image. There seemed to be no reflection at all of her reality in his gaze, though sometimes her body would come into focus, which inspired in him not empathy, but desire.


Desire to act on her, to bring her in, to envelop her, to subsume her in the image of himself.

Less and less real…

Have I lied myself into dream so entirely that my entire dreamworld is, in fact, one great delusion? And Trevor in this life—husband, father—a delusion based on a plagiarized version of the man I imagine I should be, but am not? A grotesque facsimile of a man?

He wouldn’t talk, and when he did, how much of it was muddied truth or controlling fabrications? She would find out this way, then.

The line between Sarah and Trevor.


The line between okay and not okay.


The line between husband and wife.

Making Twilight


by Trevor Hart


“Tell me your dreams,” Sara whispered.


I closed my eyes, and began: “We awoke. A four-poster bed in a medieval castle. Not us, as we are now, but dressed as though we belonged there and then, speaking a language I could not understand. There was a hint of menace. Terror lurking, exciting us. You were wearing a white dress, the bodice beaded with pearls that looked like tiny moons, emitting a white light even in the darkness. Your lips were blood-red, hands dirty, as though they’d been scraping mud, but your dress bore no smudge.


“I was wearing a heavy burgundy tunic, belted at the waist, with wolf fur adorning neck and cuffs, shod in calfskin-leather knee boots. On my belt hung a thick ring with an iron key. You held my hand as I led you down stone stairs into darkness, my other hand holding a flaming torch, burning fat acrid in my throat.


“Iron hinges creaked as I opened the barred door, and you went into the cell willingly, your eyes flickering in the torchlight, as though begging me to play honourably; uncertainty mixed with desire—my captive.


“I closed the door, lifted the key from the ring, and locked you in. Then turned and scaled the stairs, looking back at you—the pearls in your dress a shimmering twilight.”


The story went on, but Sarah found she had no taste for it.


Writing as though her master!


Her insecure husband with his limp socks and plagiarized dream stories.


He had borrowed this vignette from her!


The cruel embellishments were his, but the scene, and the costumes, the castle and dungeon, those were from a story she had shared with him in their early days, when she had been in the habit of writing fiction.


“Tell me a bedtime story, Sarah,” he had begun asking her each night. And she would, and the next morning, he would recount his dream, plucking things from her stories—Sarah delighted that he was so rapt that her imagination made its way into his dreamworld!


The two of them, building a dreamworld together.


When she had first read 
Dream the Text Like a Persistent Certainty
, she had felt so much a part of it! So joyous to see bits of her stories woven through his. His clever word-tricks, his devotion to his own literary idols so clear in his work, with embellishments and genius all his own, transforming her stories to something great.


But something else was stirring now, a hot, rageful feeling. As though something essential had been taken from 
her that she had not consented to giving, debased by the cruelty he enacted upon his captive, hopeful Sara.


Captive. Smudged. Begging. Twilight. What was Sarah if she gave so much of herself to Trevor, and this was what he made of her? Seeing her entrapment, revelling in his trickeries. Caring not at all what he inflicted.


Crack!



				Sarah’s head whipped forward then lashed back as the car came to an abrupt stop. Its forward momentum halted by the post of the 
PARENT PICKUP PARKING
 sign at Amelie’s school, which was now embedded in the front of her car.


Her hand went to the back of her neck. “Fuck!” she grunted. “How fucking embarrassing!”


Emma Ridley was staring at her, mouth agape, her son Marshall tucked in tight against her hip. Amelie and Amelie’s teacher, Ms. Singh, were there too, just two metres away from the post—Amelie with her hand over her mouth, Ms. Singh with her hand reaching toward Amelie, and then firmly grasping Amelie’s arm, holding her back.


There were also other kids and other parents, but Sarah didn’t register them as individuals, just a crowd of people. A crowd of people witnessing her major fuck-up.


“Get it together, Sarah,” she said to herself, not allowing herself to feel the impact of what had just happened, instead propelling herself to action, knowing the feelings would render her as useless as the crumpled car.


“Not now, not now, now is not the time to feel,” she rasped, tears beginning to wet her eyes, hot fast fear-despair rising in her esophagus. She opened the car door, inhaled a quick gasp, filling her lungs with air and then spitting it out again, pulled herself upright, tucked her chin in, internally repeating, “Breathe, breathe, breathe. In a-a-a-a-a-and out. In a-a-a-a-a-and out. One breath in, and gently out. Breathing in, and breathing out.”


She repeated it three times, the same words she said to herself when the kids were mid-tantrum and losing it. A life hack she had posted on her website after extensive research. A self-corrective, though her readers didn’t know the half of it, following the pony-throwing breakdown.


Sarah pictured her website banner, imagining herself sliding the cursor over to the 
Toolkit
 drop-down that popped into several side menus, one of which was titled 
Thirty Second Quickies
 that were meant to help parents stay centred and calm when the kids were out of control instead of having breakdowns bigger than those of their children.


The breathing calmed her. This was not the few notches of nervous-system activation she needed to come down from during recurring fights over whose turn it was with the Playmobil horses, but an actual potential crisis in which she could have hit a child, maybe even her own child, and killed them instead of just impaling her car with a post!


Imagining it made her nerves kick in again, so she brought up the banner once more—visualizing the cursor 
hovering over 
Toolkit
 drop-down, 
Thirty Second Quickies
 tab, 
Not an Emergency
 sub-submenu while she repeated “This is not an emergency. This is not an emergency” in her soft gentle Mommy voice to herself.


Sarah walked to the front of the car in not-an-emergency mode as though a person, as though a person in a body moving in the world. And then the slow-motion world clicked back into real-time and Ms. Singh was there with her hand on Sarah’s arm, her warm schoolteacher eyes full of concern—one hand on Amelie, one hand on Sarah as Amelie screamed, “Mama! Mama? Are you okay?” Arms around Sarah’s waist.


And Sarah brought her hand down to Amelie’s back, feeling warmth pass from her to Amelie, remembering her role. Her role to comfort Amelie, who was scared to see her mother crash semi-hard into a post at her school, terrified that things were falling apart because she was six and things were always falling apart and it was terrifying, and this time it was her mom making the world unsafe. Her mom—the one who was supposed to keep things together. Sarah wanted to assure Amelie that it was all okay, that it was not all falling apart, that Mama was here, a warm loving version of Mama who could offer comfort even though it 
was
 all falling apart inside and the car crashing into the signpost was making that visible to everyone around her and now she would have to play the scene in such a way that seamed everything back together so no one could tell.


What the fuck was wrong with her? Why couldn’t she just be a normal mom like Emma Ridley?


“It’s okay, baby,” Sarah said, “Mama’s foot slipped from the brake to the gas pedal. It’s just a dent in the car, no big deal. But Mama has to let Papa know he has to pick up Arlynn—I’ll just send him one quick text, to make sure he gets there in time.”


Sarah snapped a photo of the signpost embedded in the car and sent it to Trevor along with a text that read: 
Crashed car into sign. Everything OK! Need you to pick up Arlynn ASAP. At school with Amelie. Meet you at home later.


Jesus fuck Sarah sure yer ok
 Trevor texted back, neglecting his usual punctuation and spelling protocol. A rare response on a Monday—so he did check his messages on Mondays, after all, Sarah noted, just neglected to respond to her texts on this special day in which he’d instructed her not to expect it because he didn’t have time to check his phone. Fucker. She had known it all along.


Fine. Please get Arlynn now! Take her home, and text me when you get there, to say it’s all good. Talk after!


She had to be explicit in her instructions, she knew, otherwise Trevor would ask what his next steps should be, or worse, he would get Arlynn and then show up at the school and then Sarah would have to deal with the car, and Amelie, and Trevor, and a preschooler, and she was sure there was no way she’d be able to hold it all together then.




———


How much effort it took, upholding this massive delusion!


And now it was all falling apart!


As though a spell’s magic was failing.

And Marshall responded, “That’s not what it’s called, Mommy!”

safe

“I love you, sweetheart, have fun and I’ll see you soon, soon, soon,” Sarah whispered softly to Amelie, kissing the top of her head, running her hand down Amelie’s long auburn hair, with just the same glinting tints of flame that Trevor’s had had before it had started fading to a washed-out copper.


Ms. Singh stayed with Sarah while she sorted out the details of the tow truck. The insurance company would send a driver as soon as they could, who would take it to the nearest shop for an assessment. Sarah got out her phone to get a Lyft, but Deepa wouldn’t hear of it.


“Sarah, let me just get you home, it’s really no trouble at all.”


And Sarah’s resolve slackened, just a bit, because here she was, being looked out for by others—first Emma, and now Ms. Singh. Instead of judging her harshly, these women were helping her! Helping her and her kids be safe.


“Sure, Deepa, thank you. I appreciate it. I really do. I just don’t want to put you out of your way. Already you’re home late from work! On account of me and my stupid slip-up!” Sarah responded as she collapsed into the passenger seat of Deepa’s lovely powder-blue Prius, the last car left in the staff parking lot.


“Stop being so hard on yourself, Sarah!” Deepa scolded her, starting up her almost silent car. “It was an accident. No one was hurt—other than your car, and the sign that should probably be a bit further back from the curb anyway! We’ve all done something like that at some point. I just want to make sure you’re okay.”


And then Sarah looked at Deepa, really looked, as Deepa was looking at her. Looked without looking away to somewhere else, as she and Trevor had been doing for most of the last—how many years? As Sarah did most of the time these days with everyone but her kids, since talking to actual people in the real world was so much more complicated than writing website content, blog posts, and comments on social media feeds.


When was the last time Sarah had spent time with anyone she would actually consider a friend? God, she missed Ariadne!


In Deepa’s gaze a floodgate opened, because she wasn’t just asking in order to be kind, even though she was. She was asking because she was genuinely concerned, and could see Sarah’s anxiety rippling underneath the tight set of her jaw and quick darting eyes, all the brightness gone out of 
them, just a pretend jauntiness that looked, to anyone actually looking, like quick-firing nerves.


The floodgate was actually just a sigh. A real deep belly sigh. A sigh to end all sighs that Sarah let float out into the embryonic space of Deepa’s hybrid. “No, Deepa, I don’t actually think I’m okay. I don’t think I’m okay at all.”


“No, it doesn’t seem like you are, Sarah,” replied Deepa. “A lot of us aren’t sometimes and pretend to be.”


“I think my marriage is falling apart,” Sarah admitted. “I think it has been for a long time and I have been pretending it isn’t, or that if I just found the right formula, the right glue, the right Instructable, I might be able to stick it back together, if not exactly in the right way, then at least in some kind of way that isn’t disastrous.”


Sarah slumped into the Prius’s contoured passenger seat, exhaling so deeply it was as though all the breath she’d been holding, all her unspoken words, her unthinkable thoughts, were releasing themselves in the cozy chamber of another human’s accepting warmth. Then Sarah cringed, wrapped her arms around herself, fearing Deepa would reach over, open the passenger door from the inside and shove Sarah out for having so invaded her sanctuary. Chapter Eight

Open Your Ears

this

Sarah’s fault. Sarah ruining it all.

Swan Mom.

Sarah’s truth

Cherry


It was all said and done. Sarah slid herself into the driver’s seat of her brand-new AWD Prius, put her hands on the wheel, met her own eyes in the rear-view, and said aloud, “Here we go, Cherry.”


Before going home, she pulled Cherry to a stop in front of her favourite bakery, watching the sleek red reflection waver in the front window, one eye still seeing a bit oddly 
from the wreckage, watching her image morph as she stepped out of the car, so that instead of seeing Sarah in her flower-print capri mom tights and oversized black T-shirt, she saw another Sarah in a slinky dress, almost dripping off her body. Clinging red velvet, six-inch heels with ribbon lacing like ballet slippers wrapped up to her knee, striding and not giving a fuck. Another body emerging into the world from within the one she was seeing through.


Just a momentary flash in the window, morphing quickly back into her mom garb, opening the door, hearing the bell jingle, walking to the counter for a baker’s box of croissants and Danishes for her and the girls.


She wished Ariadne could see her pulling her stealthy red beast into the driveway, traversing the threshold, holding the box of pastries like a haughty server above her head, making a show of dropping her key fob in the basket on the ledge where she always dropped her keys, so that Trevor would see her doing it.


She had already decided she would say nothing. She was done talking. She was going to leave it to Trevor to decide how he was going to take up the subject of the replacement car, anticipating that he would see her putting the fob down and immediately understand when it fell into the basket.


But Trevor didn’t notice the gesture because he was used to seeing Sarah drop her keys in the basket and wasn’t paying attention to what she was doing, or thinking about the fact that they no longer had a car. He was otherwise occupied, prepping a snack for the girls, and couldn’t hold space in 
his mind for snack-preparation and gesture-observation at the same time.


“Croissants! Mommy bought croissants!” Amelie shouted. “I want one, I want one.”


“I want the cherry one!” Arlynn exclaimed.


“No, I want the cherry one!” Amelie retorted.


“Girls, there are enough cherry ones for all of us!” Sarah chimed in, putting out plates for them, setting the pastries down, making big googly eyes at the girls, squealing in delight as she bit in, tearing with her canines, ripping into the flesh, allowing the goo inside to ooze down her chin, declaring, “Mama’s a hungry badger! She’s eating up a tasty squirrel!”


“Eww, Mama! You have squirrel blood on your chin!” Amelie whinnied, delighted.


“Mama ate a squirrel!”—Arlynn.


At which point Trevor turned around, incredulous. “Sarah! That’s revolting! You’re teaching them to eat like animals! Giving them sweets before dinner while I’m busting my—” he paused, considering the girls, “making them a 
healthy

“Lighten up, Trevor,” Sarah quipped—too polite to quite be a growl, using her finger to wipe up the cherry goop, licking it off, then adding, prim and proper, “Girls, you can have a squirrel-blood Danish as long as you promise to eat Papa’s healthy snack after.”

Trevor arrived in the kitchen, hands gripping at his hair, imploring, “Are you sure you haven’t seen it, Sarah? It’s the only one missing! And my multi-tool is nowhere to be found!”


Before Sarah could answer, Amelie shouted from the living room, “There’s a car in our driveway! A car the same colour as the apples on Applejack!”


Trevor went over to look, and immediately understood—not only that Sarah had bought a new car, but that she had been playing some kind of game with him all night that he hadn’t known she was playing—so he was not just incredulous, but deeply enraged.


His body shook, burbling with the familiar sensation of routine marital hate. It flared from his fingertips to his throat, rippling through his skin, tightening his lips, landing in his eyes, so when he looked at Sarah, he felt not just this moment of loathing, but all the moments of loathing built up like a thick wall of burning bricks over the years he had been forced to live with her. Her petty, stupid games. This adversary who had infiltrated his house and poisoned the mind of his wife, who he now had to subjugate.

Sarah broke the gaze. “That’s our new car, kiddos! Her name is Cherry!” She turned again to look straight at Trevor with a gleeful smile that scorched all the space between them before Trevor could spit out the poison inside. “Who wants to go out and see?”

The girls rushed out the front door with Sarah at their heels while Trevor shot out the back, pulled his bike out of the shed, and sped away, down the driveway past the red abomination and a gaggle of giddy girls.

He thought he had cooled down enough on his ride to face the energy field of home, but it all reached toward him as he dismounted and set his feet on the slabs of slate paving-stone that Sarah had hauled home from one of their camping trips, broken into shards, and arranged into the shape of a sunflower that now pierced through his pores, siphoning the life force out of him, like all her fucking decorations!

He had never seen Sarah in a pair of heels in his life.


Was she trying to seduce him?


Now?


God, she looked hot!


Why couldn’t she dress like this more often?


He stopped in his tracks, eyeing her.


Why did this remind him of the porn-Troy video?

Praise


After that first class, Masha had considered dropping the course. She had felt so confident writing her piece. It had just come to her—as soon as Dr. Hart dropped the prompt. Washing out her preoccupation about whether or not he’d seen the video.


But her confidence had faltered after she’d handed in her paper, feeling as though she’d just given up something she was eager to give, but also feeling like it had been coaxed out of her. An uncanny feeling.


Still with her as she walked out of the classroom, head down, chewing her lip, confused by the sudden jittery feeling when the paper had transferred from her hand to his. His hand loomed large in her mind’s eye, like his skin pores were magnified and she could see right down to his hair follicles. Distracted, she bumped into another student, knocking the book they were holding to the ground.


“Sorry. I’m so sorry,” Masha said, bending to pick it up. “I wasn’t looking.”


It was a slim volume of poetry with a beautiful dragon on the cover. The dragon looked strikingly similar to Masha’s snake tattoo, like her snake had suddenly grown legs and head frills. But so much more brilliant, with flecks of gold on some of the scales—colours muted out in skin that stopped the eye on paper. Masha wished her skin could shine so boldly.


A rushing-water feeling washed out the jittery one when Masha locked eyes with the student, handing the book back.


“Your book,” said Masha.


“Thanks—no.”


“No?”


“I mean, no, I’m sorry. I wasn’t looking, either.”


Then the student gathered the book up to their chest and sort of bolted. Masha was struck, her gaze transfixed, until she lost sight of them in the crowded hallway.


“One more class, Masha. You have to give it at least one more class,” she whispered to herself. She was curious what the other students had written about, and some part of her wanted feedback.


And maybe she’d see this student again, if they had a class nearby at the same time?




———


Dr. Hart had been so positive about Masha’s water piece! It had made her feel a little glow inside. Perhaps she had pegged him wrong? Maybe he hadn’t seen the video. And what difference did it make if he had? He hadn’t shown any lack of composure since that first instance.


He was her teacher. So what if he watched porn? Most men did. And men who weren’t gay wanted to have sex with women. There were rules about when, where, and who they were supposed to have it with, to make it seem like they didn’t always want to. This was not a Dr. Hart assignment. Dr. Hart would never assign his students the task of writing about being haunted, drawing on intersectional and women writers to do so. Chapter Nine


Textual Movement




Transference


Sarah inserted a memory tampon into the proper hole and let “Merriment” seep into its absorbent fibres. She pulled on the string and popped the tampon out, padding down to Trevor’s office.


It slipped right into Trevor’s drive as though not soaked in the memory blood of her desktop, bleeding “Merriment” into Trevor’s 
Work in Progress
 folder.


No matter how she told the story, he would read it from his own isolated perspective, or not read it all, pulling the blanket back over his eyes so he did not have to look at truths he had the privilege not to be made to see. So long as he had a Sara who’d come and lift the tiniest corner, hand

Sara was tired.


Sarah wanted to give her a break.


Trevor’s blanket was too small and wrapped too tightly around him. There was no room inside for Sara. She needed some nourishment too, and some warm entreaties. A place to hide away, where someone would sometimes come and help her to find her way out when she got scared. Sarah would give her those, so Sara did not have to be stuck in a mystical loop, walking through the same scenes over and over again, forever being mystically, achingly unreachable.


My Body in Three Acts


Mid-term Assignment CW 101


Dr. Trevor Hart




My Body in Three Acts


by Masha Novak


Act 1: Formation


I remember the space between non-being and being.


Half unmanifest.


A hollow where snakes writhed and I was the space between their bodies.


 




———


Where does memory live? In the cells? In the mind? In the gaps and passageways between matter and consciousness—between roots beneath cedar—cellular matter inside grandmother’s womb.




———


Always my mother carried the possibility of me. Both threat and gift.


Girls are taught to understand there is danger in their bodies.


Danger to make life. Danger to invoke desire that is life-taking.


A delicate art to acquiesce and refuse at the same time. Tricky to simultaneously debase and uphold oneself.


Keep the secret, only to be told at the right time.


Or else have it taken unwillingly.


Efface the entire range of feeling between willing and unwilling through shame cast upon girl flesh—the horror of its emergence. The beauty, also.


Flowering/deflowering.




———


I remember being thistle.


Spikes, sweet purple nectar.


Sweetness and danger, both.


Does a girl smell her own pungency before other forces act to take from her what she may never learn to feel as her own essence? Drawing toward herself bees that tickle so gently, not harming her in the process? Transforming her to seed-bearing fruit.


Bees impregnate flowers with the sex of other flowers. Girls, if they know what’s good for them, do everything they can to prevent impregnation.




———


I formed too soon for my mother. Memory gives me nothing but a gap where story slips in.


Am I writing about my mother’s body or my body? If my mother were able to tell a true story about her body, how would she tell it?


It is beyond my power in life to redeem my mother’s body, though not beyond my power in word. I remember being in my mother’s body, but I do not remember my mother. She gave birth to a baby the world would not uphold her to keep.




———


I was formed of my father’s desire. From the heat and the friction of his body in my mother’s body.


Did my mother want my father back? This I will never know.


I remember divinity and I remember desecration.




———


I do not remember my father’s body.


I do not remember my father.




———


The mother I came to know found me. My body a thread woven into story. My body as rift and gift both.


I remember ice. Cold on my tongue, binding me to cold metal bars. I remember pain as heat melded my skin to the 
steel’s cold surface. Frozen hands in snow, my mother’s eyes. How I loved her more than anything, and hated her for everything she could not give. But wanted her love more than anything, and so formed myself to please her, my wants merging with hers.


I remember hate, and I remember love.


I remember joy at the outreaching of my body. The way it spread as speed in the world, careening toward all manner of discoveries. Hurts happened—skinned knees, gravel embedded in palm-skin, bruises, scabs to be picked at and re-bloodied. But they were small, temporary sufferings. My body was joy. Glorious happening. It knew the world. The world spoke to it, and it, in turn, spoke back by the force of bones encased in skin that knew the gentle rub of caterpillar feet and the tickle of discovered feathers. Skin turned copper in the summer sun. The cold weight of stones become hot in my hands, hurled into water, reverberating ripples that made lily pads dance and frogs hop into the safety of their underwater worlds. I ate from the world—berries dripping down my chin. I flourished.


Act 2: Sexualization


I remember betrayal.


My body’s or the world’s?


I flourished. My body invoked desire I did not desire. Other bodies wanted to take what was mine, whether or not I desired to share.


Some things I could not hide.


My power was to refuse.


Many tried to deny me this power.


Some succeeded.


I remember I had my own desires. Sometimes clear, sometimes muddy. I remember sometimes I desired and what I received was not what I desired.


Sometimes I did not refuse because I felt I owed a response. And what I learned to give felt like estrangement. Making 
consent
 an empty word—easier to consent than bear the psychic or physical brunt of refusal. Body untethered from mind. Mind watching body give, feeling nothing back. Tiny pleasure streaks rising from places that were always improperly touched on the way to male release, and then hazed over. I watched as though a long way off, pretending closeness.


Gaps haunt, blur memory, connections sever in the blur.


I remember gaps between snakes coiled together in an underground hibernaculum—space I could always recede to.


I remember I danced, training the mind to ignore the pain of the body in order to push through to form. Pain transformed into an articulation of grace through serious repetitive effort. Pain in order to build memory in the body, until mastery, and then no longer pain, but expression of will from within. Consciousness no longer needed.


I remember I lost myself in desire—subsumed in ambition to become the bodies of dancers I admired. Want and will entwined. Thousands of minute repetitions 
layered into choreographed memory amalgamated to performance—remembered beyond memory, re-enacted. Executed with spirit.


I lost spirit when my body betrayed me, granting me breasts and hips and an ass that unbalanced. I began to stumble.


For years progressing toward ideal form, then my body un-becoming my dreams, betraying them with its own intentions, transforming me without my bidding. Making a mockery of my grace, smudging out body memory as a new body emerged—a facsimile of the female form fed back to me as model. Uninhabitable. Not dancing, just a thing on display.




———


I remember my mother’s wants changed over time. She wove me in, and then the weft unravelled, and I was left behind with my father.


I remember my father sent me away.


Act 3: Fornication and my Fairy Stepmother


I remember boys whose faces I don’t always remember—wanted and unwanted hands and kisses carved into memory like slashes in shed skin.


I remember my fairy stepmother.


Possessed of her own unhidden wants.


Drawing me from my hiding place.




———


Inhabiting her body as celestial form, teaching me to wear my own skin.


Gaps as entry points rather than terrible abysses.


Teaching me to become curious—margins in the gaps, interior experiences under the radar. Aware within oneself.


When I write about my fairy stepmother, transgression looms. She could be read as corruption. But what she offered was absolution. Inviting me to witness desire as celebration. Sharing with me art forms in which women’s pleasure was an organizing principle rather than gaping exclusion or signifier of shame. Inviting me to ask questions, to speak, to voice my own desires.


I remember learning to curate my own experience within the gaze. Which shook apart the story.


Is it corruption to shake a corrupt story?




———


For a girl to change the choreography of a myth?




———


Masha!


This piece is an anthem. Your voice is a punch to the gut and a feather down the cheek at the same time. You understand what to do with repetition! I reread it three times, and then reread it once more. You tackle difficult stuff with honesty and bravado! I have some thoughts I’d like to share with you about pacing and style, could you come into office hours this week?


Professor T.


Correspondence


Two letters slipped through time to land on Sarah’s floor. One from Ariadne on her trademark embossed stationery, and another, penned in stunning calligraphy that made Sarah ache to get out her own stylus and try her hand at the spirit flowing through the letters of her own name.




———


She opened the second letter reverently, not knowing who had written it.




———


Then dissociated from the world for the amount of time it takes for a myth to unravel itself.




———


She returned to life when her fingers registered a texture she recognized underneath the myth-breaking letter—Ariadne’s stationery. A warmth emanating from it, reaching through a broken story to call Sarah back.




———


Ariadne’s letter contained fragments for a memoir Ariadne was working on—bits of source material and reflections on archaeological research methods, musings about time and scientific data proving arsenic poisoning from water sources had led to staggering rates of infant mortality among the Chinchorro. Ancient people who, for thousands of years, made mummies of babies that had died too young, carrying them forward in life, making daily company of their dead.


Water as both life source and cause of premature death. Complex mourning rituals for living and dying on land that gave as much as it took—mummies as relics of perpetual grief. Death a constant feature of life, rather than a disgusting secret no human should be subject to except for entertainment purposes—violence supposedly contained in commodified fantasy worlds.


Layers of multiple truths.


How did Ariadne always know?


Real. Unreal. Ariadne was enmeshed in the same questions as Sarah, only she came at it so differently! Analyzing stages of flesh decay, climate change, rainfall, bacterial threat, skull preservation, desiccation techniques, and flayed sea lion skin woven through with fine needlework using human hair, with which the Chinchorro patched together facsimiles of dead kin. Ancestral ghosts as symbolically alive as the living.


Dear Sarah,


I’m including what I know is a mess of notes! I need your help! I know you already have so much on your plate. But the idea keeps coming up for me so I’m floating it to you. Co-write the memoir with me! I am planting the seed. You have always helped me speak my scattered thoughts into a coherent language.


Sarah’s heart moved within her ribcage. Shifted, she was sure of it, opening a chamber she had sealed shut. She 
felt blood surge, washing out the settling pond her heart had become, surviving on whatever backwaters came its way. A vibration that made her limbs tremble.


Shaking Trevor’s stories and the myth-breaking letter out.


Sarah lay down on her bed, hand over Ariadne’s letter, letter over heart, everything cohering—all her interwoven selves.


Wetness emerged from her eyes, forming water droplets, saline and heavy, staining her cheeks, leaving traces like salt rivulets in a desert.


I keep having this daydream of you staying with me here with the girls while we write the book together. I know it would be hard on your end to figure out, but can we talk about it? My sabbatical starts at the end of this semester and I’ve got money from a research grant to pay you. I hope you’ll think about it. You actually being here would help so much with the texture of the dreams that come unbidden in this landscape. Well, I won’t say too much about those now!


Yours always,


Ariadne


Sarah went through the notes feverishly. They were disordered, but as soon as she’d spread them out on her bedroom floor, a thread emerged. She could see how she would write Ariadne’s story. She could feel without doubt that in doing so, she would also bring life back to her own.


She pictured it as though from a bird’s-eye view.


She and Trevor in the tourist hedge maze in Bavaria, Trevor dropping the ball. The two of them running in haste through the exit, emerging to find the black swans.


Sarah had lived that story.


She looked again, then scrawled on the back of Ariadne’s envelope:




———


Once upon a time, Sarah fell asleep and woke up in the centre of a labyrinth. There were a series of signs instructing her very clearly to go in the right direction. There was a ball of thread—a strand leading back the way she knew she had come in.


Sarah picked up the thread, turned around, and followed it, retracing her steps, winding the thread up into a ball full of dust and cobwebs gathered from all the years of lying on a labyrinth floor. Knowing she would emerge not into the past holding a fabled clew, but to a place where she had many choices to make.


She felt a tug on the string, and looking up, she saw her children in front of her, holding the thread in their hands. They had picked it up, and were walking with her, following the thread in the same direction as she wound it up behind them. Ahead of them, almost disappearing beyond each turn before Sarah could see her, was another girl wearing a red cape, just a figment, this one, but she was with them too, astonished to be coming out into the light after all this time.

Chapter Ten


Winding Up




Back In Class


Let us not pretend the way out was as straightforward as simply following a thread or crossing a threshold.


Sarah banged into walls.


Got lost in halls of mirrors, baffled by her own reflection.


Threw the ball of thread as far away from her as she could, hoping for it to explode.


She wept.


And raged.


Curled her head into her hands, making herself into a little seed on the floor, unmoving.


Got up.


Because life.


Took care of the children as though minutes before she had not been curled up on the floor.


Sarah had plans to make. She made them between tears that felt like parts of her dying.

Showing up in Trevor’s class in fishnets and a miniskirt, black leather boots up to her knees. Hair plaited in the middle, two French braids swirled like pink-and-black candy canes to her shoulders. Breath smelling of orange Tic Tacs, lips tasting of cherry Chap Stick, sitting down next to Masha.

“Masha. I haven’t seen you before—you’re joining halfway through the semester?”


“Just checking it out. I’ve heard compelling things about the prof.”

In class, Masha would be a student. Not a porn actor. Jenny would get the difference. Would Masha find Jenny interesting? Would she want to be friends with her?


Masha might pause then, looking at Jenny, uncertain, pupils expanding black saucers.


Jenny would ask, concerned, “Are you okay?”


“Yeah,” Masha might respond, looking away. Maybe not wanting to be friends if Jenny was only there to check out the prof.




———


Trevor would walk into the room, not seeing Jenny at first, eyes drawn to Masha.


Double-taking.


“Hi Professor T.,” Jenny would pipe up, uncrossing her legs, spreading them wide, bringing her fingers between her knees, up through her miniskirt, making a show of it. Not pretending it was any other way.


“I revised the poem you gave me feedback on. It’s a performance piece now. It requires your participation,” Jenny would say, slipping her fingers in between a gap she had made in the strings.


Everyone in class turning to stare at the spectacle.


At which point Jenny would get up, give Professor T. a wink, and, slow as oil, saunter out the way she had slithered in, leaving Trevor to do whatever he would do in the aftermath.


Reckoning


Sarah could not risk Trevor finding out. She knew he would do everything he could to derail her.


“I am their father; I have a right to decide!” he would say. Or, “What madness is this, Sarah? There is no way you can do this alone. I’ll be damned if I’m helping finance 
this escapade!” Or, failing more overt tactics: “I’ll do better, I promise. I’ve just lost my way, I need you. I want to change. I will change. I’ll prove to you I can. You know I can’t do this without you.”


How many times had Sarah heard these words, believing them, hope building for the duration of time in which Trevor tried, crashing when he gave up trying, because it was “all too goddamn much, Sarah. These constant unending demands.”


Why should he be granted the right to decide, given that he spent all of half an hour engaging with the girls directly on any given day, maybe a few hours on the weekend, as long as he was not distracted by other things that needed his full attention? Had he earned this right, as Sarah had? Earned the right by basing almost every one of her decisions for the past decades of her life on Trevor’s and then the girls’ needs? On what was best for 
them
.


Did he have the right to decide, given that he was so obsessed with a student he believed might be the one for him because she was the star of a porn video that had found him on the internet? Did he have a right to decide, given the letter that had so artfully spelled things out, that she could not pretend away, as she had so many of Trevor’s betrayals?


Sarah did not believe Trevor deserved the right, but the effort it would take to prove it to others was beyond her at the moment. Still, she had to get out, and she had to take 
the girls with her because she was afraid of what Trevor would do once the walls of the castle truly crumbled.


How many times? How many times had he crossed the line?


How could this man guide the life of his daughters?


He had hurt Sarah. Had hurt her repeatedly. Hurt her and hurt her and hurt her without ever taking responsibility. Always managing somehow to shift the blame to her. And Sarah, wanting to see Trevor’s perspective, knowing she was fallible, had accepted it. Because there was truth in his words too. But could everything be Sarah’s fault all the time? Was Trevor blameless?


Believing he existed in the world to do what was important to him, and when he wasn’t consumed with that, satisfying his urges by consuming whatever he came across that aroused—eating his fill of women’s beauty, abandoning them to deal with the debris. His shit, which he would take no responsibility for. She knew. She knew well enough. She who had lived so long charged with his toilet, his yucky words, his smears.

How disturbed Sarah had been after the first time seeing the video. The twin impulse to recoil and surge. The questions that came on the heels of the feelings—is this girl okay? Is this what she wants? Is she being coerced into this? Just acting like she really wants this? What is going on here, I mean, what is going on? Is she really in control?


Desire had washed out concern. When she was pretending to be Trevor, thoughts like these were barred from disrupting the viewing experience. But they had rushed in again when Trevor receded, when she was back to being Sarah. She could not impersonate Trevor forever.


Sarah knew there were people behind the cameras, and she had worried that they were taking advantage. What had been going on in Masha’s body and in her mind when she put her clothes back on and left the set?


In one of their sessions, Arya suggested that these were important questions to ask, and prodded Sarah to get curious rather than turn away from her unease. Well, if there was anything Sarah knew how to do, it was follow leads.


She began to devour criticism by pornography scholars, as well as content by women working in the industry, and what they were saying about their own agency and fantasies. How they were aware of threading the line between exploitation and agency. How they played that line to serve their own interests and desires. Some of them, anyway. Those with enough agency to reflect on and articulate their experiences. Some even did it as political engagement, wanting to create sex-positivity and alternative female fantasies to the deluge of misogynistic porn that flooded the internet.


She also read critics who argued that the commodification of women’s bodies was still commodification, in service of images that served to subjugate women, no matter their experienced agency. She read the feminist philosopher 
Amia Srinivasan, who argued that “porn does not inform, or persuade, or debate. Porn trains.”


She liked what cultural critic Maggie Nelson had to say about “a different kind of freedom drive—one that longs to be self-forgetful, incautious, overwhelmed.” Was struck by Nelson quoting sexuality scholars Lauren Berlant and Lee Edelman, that sometimes in sex there was a “desire to be non-sovereign, and sometimes not-autonomous.”


Imagine writing about this on her mom blog! It was so off-brand! Though she did get a kick thinking about a 
Porn for Sexually Uninterested Moms
 drop-down.


It was funny, and it wasn’t. Because there was so much exploitation, and so much shame. Complex, intertwined, and prevalent. People bought in and pretended not to. According to Srinivasan, reports had shown that young boys were accessing online porn at the average age of eleven, and that most boys now believed sex 
was
 porn, approaching their lovers’ bodies as though teenage girls were porn stars who wanted to be fucked the way the free online porn they could get their hands on depicted men fucking them. Which was often brutal. Misogynistic fantasies played out on the screen. And in this way, they were being trained into their sexual selves as brutalizers rather than lovers.


This part sickened Sarah. Is this what had happened to Trevor? What had made him such a dissociated lover? Requiring so much emotional labour from her to bring him into a place of connection to have the kind of sex she desired.


She wanted to redeem all these young men. Give them porn that honoured women’s bodies and desires; if they were going to be watching porn anyway, whether or not their parents wanted them to, shouldn’t they be watching kinder forms of it? Forms that gave the opportunity for different kinds of bodies to demonstrate different kinds of pleasures?


Oh, she was glad her kids were so young still, but the time would come when they would want phones, enticement, self-forgetful experiences, and sex. There was no stopping that. But she wanted so much more for her girls’ bodies than for them to be acted upon as porn bodies by the boys and men they might seek someday to know. Just thinking of this made her wish her girls would seek other girls’ bodies instead, or the bodies of anyone who did not identify with the male bodies of free pornlandia. She wanted her girls to seek out bodies of other beings who would show them tenderness, with whom any act would be held in tenderness, even if there was wild energy at the core of it—energy that spun with gale force, sweeping bodies up into freneticism and pounding—still, for there to be tenderness harmonizing between the bodies—tethering being to being in generosity rather than rapacious hunger.




———



				Sarah read, absorbing. There was so much to take in. She was not alone, she knew. There were so many others wanting, hoping, fearing, desiring, in bodies akin to hers. Wanting to be honoured and honourable rather than desecrated and 
desecrating, sometimes learning right by learning wrong, and sometimes that learning took so much longer than was good.


Sarah wanted her children’s learning to be easier than hers had been. She knew most parents did. How could she guide them in a world full of men whose desires were mired in a sense of fragile superiority imposed by misogynistic porn and competition with other men—desires manufactured in repetitive media loops that tripped brain wires to make men believe it was all a game of pursuit and subjugation. That to be proper sexual beings, men had to impose their wills on bodies with less social power in some kind of supposedly mutually desired performance. It was one way to do sex, and sometimes Sarah had wanted this kind of play with Trevor, but she had wanted so many other kinds too, broader roles—for him to be okay trying different things instead of feeling threatened by anything that fell outside the script. As her body changed and needs shifted, Sarah had grown tired and turned off by the limitations and collateral storylines this scenario required of her.


It began at home, she knew. She and Trevor were performing the future stories their children would subconsciously seek to re-enact. If the girls saw their mother take the shit—take it over and over and over again, her whole being recoiling in protest, contorting itself to try to make life livable while their father went about re-enacting the same damaging stories—then what stories would they come to believe in for themselves?


Sarah did not have the power to change the ways in which Trevor told his stories.


But she could choose whether or not to play with him.


Professor T.


Masha was deeply ambivalent about Dr. Hart’s feedback.


The directions had been confounding. And her piece was so personal.


Why did it feel important to demonstrate to this man what it felt like on the other side? To assume that he would read, and in reading make a move to shift his perspective to allow her point of view to hold space in his mind?


She wanted him to see, but she didn’t really want to hear his perspective. She didn’t need to. She already knew his perspective. There was no way to avoid knowing Dr. Hart’s perspective. It was everywhere.


Still, she could not deny that there was a draw. Something enticing about the way he looked at her. It stirred her uncomfortably. Why did his approval even matter? Not just matter, but make her feel more worthy somehow? God, she wished her prof were someone she felt she could trust instead of someone who seemed to want something from her.


She wasn’t in class to be sexualized, she was there to learn, to be taken seriously. His praise for her work felt baited—little hooks hidden under pretty words. Hard to actually believe.


Yet she admired him, too. She had read his work. She 
had found his stories fascinating. He was erudite, surprising. He swept a reader up. His turns of phrase had made her laugh, while also offering depth, questioning the line between fantasy and reality, never quite offering resolution.


Did he really like her work? Or did he just want to win her over?


Masha wasn’t so naïve as to believe she was truly unique in his eyes. That she was as special as he seemed to want her to feel she was. The energy was too calculated. Too precise. She had enough experience with men to understand a lascivious look when she met one. She had plans for herself and her work that did not include being a professor’s young plaything.


Yet still, how enticing for one’s uniqueness to be held in the light of one they admired; it 
did
 stir her!


Reading the passages Dr. Hart had assigned as prompts, she had felt herself float away, underlining almost every second line. She knew she was in the presence of greatness, reading these works. The Harjo poem had made her weep. As though someone she knew were in it, speaking to her. Masha did remember. Her being remembered. She remembered, but was also lost.


Masha was grateful to be gifted readings she would not have found on her own, to be in the presence of greatness, and Dr. Hart had made this possible for her. But she was not grateful that Dr. Hart wanted to discuss her essay with her. She should have thought that this was where it might lead when she turned it in. Because that was his role, after all. But 
she hadn’t thought about him or the other students when she’d written it. Only after she’d hit the send button on her email, immediately wishing she’d waited. Written something else instead. Submitted something less vulnerable, less honest. That she had kept in mind who her audience was, instead of allowing herself to become lost in her own gesture.


But it was too late. She had sent it. He had read it. And now he wanted to talk to her.

Yuna cringed when Masha showed them the note Dr. Hart had scrawled on her essay.


“He’s a creep.”


“Why do you say that?”


“He just is. He creeps on the girls in class. He’s got a new pet every few years.”


“I don’t mind being teacher’s pet, it beats being the invisible student!”


“Maybe,” Yuna replied. “I guess it depends what kind of pet you are.”


“I like to think of myself as a pelican,” teased Masha. “Mouthful of fish and a beak that could cause some real damage if rubbed the wrong way.”


Yuna didn’t laugh, just stroked Masha’s arm in a way that always made Masha melt inside. Her sparkly dragon friend—fierce with wizened humour that tore cracks in the fabric of the world so magic could find its way out. Like Ursula Le Guin’s Tehanu, the burned girl who was also a dragon. A person with whom Masha could be all her 
silly-serious, flawed, mouth-full-of-shredded-fish-parts pelican selves. Who reciprocated with their own complexities and idiosyncratic appetites rippling through eyes flecked with gold like the scales of their poetry, glittering all through Masha’s skin.

Later, Masha asked, “Yuna, would you come to office hours with me?


“I do want feedback. I want to know how to make my work better, but I don’t feel comfortable going in there alone. I don’t like the way Dr. Hart looks at me. I know what you mean about the creep factor. I want to believe it’s sincere attentiveness. That he truly sees something special in me. But I also feel like he’s looking right through me, or trying to pry into me. I can’t exactly place it, but it feels off somehow.


“And ‘Professor T.’!? I mean, I get that it upends formality, but that weirds me out.”


Yuna didn’t respond, so Masha continued, “I didn’t tell you before because I wanted to put it out of my mind, and it seemed doubtful, but I can’t shake the feeling he’s seen me on film, and that he recognizes me. That he feels like something of me belongs to him, because of what he’s seen.”


Masha hesitated. “I’ve been trying to shake it off, vacillating between dropping the class and just bearing my way 
through this semester, since it’s a pre-req for all the other classes I want to take.

When he reads my lines, I feel them differently. It’s weird. I want it to be funny, the pet thing, but honestly, I don’t feel like a pelican when I’m around him. That was when Masha noticed Yuna’s head was bowed, and Yuna’s hands were clenched together, balled up at their throat, and Yuna was very quietly heaving.

They had to say something now, or else Masha might find herself ensnared. And so, they told Masha the story of how Dr. Hart had shone his light upon them. Shone and shone and shone, until finally, Yuna shone the full force of their own light back. And then he had snuffed it out, shattering them to incomprehension.


Masha learned that Professor T. had written Yuna a similar note about their first assignment, inviting them to office hours to talk about their poem.


Poetry was everything to Yuna.


There is something very, very special about your work, Yuna,
 Dr. Hart had written. 
It’s so delicate. Your use of language—you approach it almost timidly, but then introduce fire, hinting at hidden power within.


Yuna remembered because they had written it all down in their journal. Every encounter, every word. The first two workshops had been titillating. “You are like a Flower Dragon,” Dr. Hart had said to them once, pulling words from one of their poems.


Their senior year, Yuna became hopelessly infatuated with Professor T.—which Dr. Hart insisted his favourite students call him. Yuna knew Professor T. was married, that he had a little girl and a new baby. They knew his wife had a blog. And even though Yuna wasn’t a mom, they read the blog religiously because they wanted to know everything they could about Professor T.


They often reread the post he had written about becoming a father. The tenderness, the remade identity, the deep vulnerability and fear. They wanted to gather Professor T. up in themself and hold all his tenderness. They also wanted to take him as the tender man he was, to feel their way into him, giving him something he barely knew he wanted. They looked every few weeks for his feature on his wife’s mom blog. Rapt in the tension between the distance and the closeness they could feel, growing ever closer as fall turned to winter, turned to spring, Dr. Hart pulling them closer and closer with each note slipped into

The Fire Series

A tree aglow

To which he did not respond.

And the next day, quoting Audre Lorde:


Touching you I catch midnight


as moon fires set in my throat


To which he did not respond.

And the day after that:

To which he did not respond.

Yuna returned to the tree, hoping their presence would resonate to him through the force of their connection, spending a good part of the night perched between watchfulness and dream. And the night after that, and the next night after. Refreshing their email inbox every ten minutes, waiting for his response.


In their next email, they were more direct.

And then, the next time they refreshed, there it was, the response they had been waiting for. Their heart leapt into their throat seeing his name in bold, crashing when they read his response.


Yuna, it never happened. It wasn’t real.

How many responses did Yuna write to Professor T.? A hundred, perhaps a thousand? Each one they burned.


Each one they burned.


Each one they burned.


And burned and burned.


Along with all the unreal notes he had given them.


And by the end of the burning, Yuna was there, altered by the fire, charred and yet glowing again within. After a poem that never happened and a night that never happened and the things Yuna had thought were true that weren’t, even though they were. Because Yuna lived in the real world and knew what they felt there to be true.


The poem had been written, the night had been lived, the glow and burning had compressed their spirits within a core of co-creation before flaring them out—Yuna reformed, Professor T. in convenient denial.


It was not just that Dr. Hart was a creep, Yuna understood, but a coward.


He had no guiding light, so he sought sparks in other bodies, drawing other beings’ lights into himself to light his own fire, discarding the source once his fire was burning, their 
light now diminished by the fury of his flaring. Then, when all the fuel was gone, exhausted, he turned outward, once again, seeking new light to draw him out of the darkness.


Still, even after so long, sometimes the force of the burning between them overcame. They had felt his spirit. It was there somewhere—the man corrupted, but the spirit yearning to love, as all spirits did. How sad Yuna sometimes felt, from the spirit within their own being, for the spirit within his that had to live amidst such deprivation.




———


Yuna had felt so brave that fateful day, a copy of their newly printed book in hand. Overtaken by an absurd notion that somehow, it would finally free them to look him in the eye, hand him the book, and walk away without scattering to ash.


Standing outside the classroom, their courage had begun to wane. Their body growing restless. This was a terrible idea! What were they hoping to prove? Their body was pulling them in two directions at once, wanting to get away and wanting to give the book to Dr. Hart, when providence had blessedly intervened, knocking it right out of their hands.


The sound of the book smacking the linoleum had shocked them into stasis—that old hollow coldness—their words discarded, as flat and cold as manufactured flooring.


Shivering in a tree, wordless.


But then the book had been retrieved. Handed back to them by a magnificent stranger, who had paused briefly to look closely at the cover before giving Yuna back their words. Looking at Yuna with warmth, kindness, and curiosity—in 
blunt contrast to the skittish way she had just rushed out of Dr. Hart’s classroom.


“Your book,” Yuna heard.


Then heard themself mutter, “Thanks—no.”


“No?” the stranger asked.


What the fuck am I doing here?

“I mean, no, I’m sorry. I wasn’t looking, either,” they managed.


And then they’d bolted, knowing the freeze would return if Dr. Hart were to walk out the door and lock his eyes to theirs.




———


A few days later, at the book launch, Yuna had heard a voice that drew their gaze from the page. “I recognized the cover from the display at the Student Union bookstore. I saw you were having a book launch, so I came. It’s stunning. Your poetry is stunning. Thank you.”


“Um, oh, uh, thank you?” Yuna had managed, unsure of what to say but wanting to say more. “I’m Yuna.”


“I know. It’s on the cover,” she had said, smiling radiantly. “I’m Masha.”




———


“Why didn’t you tell me before?” Masha asked, cupping her hands around Yuna’s.


“I didn’t know how. It was like the words were sucked out of me, or stuck somehow in my throat. My body knowing it had all happened, that it had all been real, my mind doubting me. Like maybe I’d made it all up. Like it was all 
my fault somehow. And if I’d just done things differently it would have gone differently. He said it had been me—that I had tricked him into it.


“I had wanted it so badly. But I didn’t go there on my own. He invited me.”


Yuna paused, and Masha could feel them being sucked back in, their whole face contorted in agony. She squeezed Yuna’s hands, smiling, to remind them they were here and now, together.


Yuna continued, “I see you. You’re real. And I can feel it all happening again. Him coaxing you when you know better. I don’t want him to use you like he used me.”


Dressed for Success


Dear Dr. Hart,


Thank you for the positive feedback on my essay. I can’t make it into your office hours, unfortunately. I’d be happy to receive any additional comments in class or by email.


All my best,


Masha Novak




———


The following week, Masha showed up to class looking like a different person. She had cut her hair short, an above-the-ear pageboy, and dyed it fluorescent red. She was dressed in a loose-fitting men’s button-up that came down past her knees. She showed no skin except on her face and neck, wearing old-fashioned white satin gloves over her hands. 
On her feet were shoes that looked so much like Trevor’s oxblood half-brogues that he did a double take. They weren’t, being sized properly to Masha’s much smaller feet, but they were damn close. They made him shiver, his own feet shrinking in on themselves. Her lips were painted a glistening purple colour. She looked like an androgynous vampire.


Trevor had never seen what he thought of as an ethnic vampire, but here Masha was in class—Eastern European, he’d assumed by the name, but now he knew she was adopted, so she was some other kind of exotic to him—unplaceable, exactly. Still, he thought, he could click the 
Ethnic
 tab to find her—and then immediately felt slightly disgusted with himself, disturbed by how his porn browsing crept so easily into his every day.


Trevor bore through the fabric on her arm to her tattoo. What was it with women and costumes? First his wife in the red dress, and now this? He knew how to see through whatever clothes they put on their bodies—to wash whatever was intended away, the immense pleasure of disrobing. He almost felt he could see two glowing orbs—the snake’s eyes—beckoning him. He chewed his gum to bring himself back, mashing away the feeling of disappointment he’d felt at the response she’d given him.


He’d offered his special attention and she had put him off. Intentional? Wanting him to work a little harder? She was tough. It made her all the more enticing.


“Masha! Hello. Welcome back to class,” he said, and then invited each of the students to read a five-minute 
excerpt from the essays they had submitted the previous week for critique.


“Remember, as you hear your classmates reading, to keep in mind that we are trying to encourage one another to find our voices. Where is the truth in this? What feels strong to you? What feels like it needs work? We will be workshopping these pieces and this will lead into our next assignment—“Flash as Form”—in which each of you will write a flash prose piece, working from the themes brought forth in your essays.”


Masha wrote in her notebook, 
Where is the truth in this?
 Circling it, and underlining three times. 
What is strongest? What needs work?
 She wanted to remember these questions as she heard her classmates read. When they did, she was able to put Professor T. out of her mind.


Floating



				The departure to Chile was less than a month off. Sarah’s first draft of Ariadne’s notes had been posted, the original document synced in a folder on her desktop and laptop, labelled 
Ariadne
. Also in the folder were copies of all their travel documents.


Sarah and the girls were headed back to Vancouver to visit Sarah’s mother.


“You were just there and you’re going back so soon? Pulling the girls out of school to visit right now? It’s unlike you to plan a last-minute trip, Sarah.”


“I know, but my mom really misses the girls, and I’m just 
remembering this summer—how good it was for both of us to have the space. I can see the end-of-the-semester grind is starting to get to you and I thought if the girls and I took a trip now, it would be good for everyone. There are a few more spots I want to hit up before I launch my Vancouver travel guide, and getting somewhere warmer for a few weeks also feels pretty appealing before winter fully sets in.”


“It all makes sense, Sarah. You should go. You should definitely go.”


“This might be just what I need,” Trevor thought. It was strange, but everything was strange right now. Summer 
had
 been good. So good. Perhaps this would be the start of longer periods of ease now that the girls were getting older, with Sarah giving him time alone to focus on his work. He could carve out a sweet creative groove, instead of spinning all the time between Sarah, Masha, and all that life demanded!




———


Although Sarah was keeping it together, fuelled by a stoic determination to see this thing through, she was harried by a persistent feeling that somehow Trevor would find out and hijack it all, roping her back into the maelstrom.


A break from the tension the charade required of her—civility and feigned playfulness cling-wrapped over deep disgust and roiling rage—was exactly what was needed. She had to bide her time until she was safe in Chile with the girls, and from there, reveal the truth to Trevor.

Amelie insisted that Grandma Grace take them first thing to visit Science World, since they had missed it the last time! And Sarah had 
promised

“I don’t want you to come, Sarah. It will be more fun without you stressing about this and that, itching to take photos. Me and the girls will just do what we do, taking our time. We’ll amble or the girls can rush around while I sit and watch.


“Just take the day to yourself. Or can you not even settle enough to take one day to not be working or doing everything for everyone else?”


“A whole day, Mom? Seriously? I don’t know what I’d do.”


“That’s the question, isn’t it, dear.”


Oh god, this was exactly how she sounded to Trevor! So annoying when someone else was always right about what you needed!


Was it still 
not working

did

Which was how she found herself wandering the seawall.
